| F112 | **A failed COMMIT was never rolled back.** Every store mutation ends `return tx_commit(s);` (11 sites) or `r = tx_commit(s)` (3), and Req 7's refusal path calls `(void)tx_commit(s)` and ignores the result outright; none of them rolled back when COMMIT itself failed (only `store_open` did, through its `fail_tx`). SQLite documents that a COMMIT failing with `SQLITE_BUSY` leaves the transaction active; on that handle every later `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` then fails, and the open transaction holds the write lock against every other connection -- the store sets no busy timeout, so a second `store_open` of the file fails at once -- until the handle closes. It fails closed -- nothing partial is committed -- so it is availability, not integrity, and the path is narrow: in WAL mode with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` the write lock is already held when COMMIT runs, and for the three real I/O failures V4-23 injected at COMMIT through a test VFS (a failed WAL write, a full disk, a failed fsync) SQLite 3.45.1 had already rolled the transaction back itself. A test-only hook fails a COMMIT with its transaction left open; for each of the 18 sweep cases that run a transaction, `test_authd_store` checks that the call fails, the committed database is unchanged, a second store can then open the file, and the same handle completes the call. The old helper failed all 18, at exactly the last two. Campaign v67 kills F112 restored (FC1) and a COMMIT made around the helper on Req 7's path (FC2) | V4-23 |

| F127 | **A frame pipelined behind another waited for more input, and pipelined bytes behind a nearly full buffer closed the connection.** Found writing V4-54's event-loop tests. The loop is half-duplex: after serving a frame whose reply is queued, it stops consuming input until the reply has gone out. But frames were served only after a read, so when the reply went out, a second frame already in the buffer waited for the peer to send something more -- and a peer that sent two frames and waited for both replies waited for its idle deadline (served 1 of 2, 9 of 18 bytes back). And every read took a whole 4096-byte chunk: pushed into a buffer that had less room, it was refused as an overflow, so two back-to-back maximum-size frames closed the connection (served 0 of 2). Every client in the repository is lock-step and never pipelines, and a peer that does harms only itself: no security impact. | v4 (V4-8a's event loop, found in V4-54) | Low | `apps/authd/evloop.c` `slot_readable`, the POLLOUT dispatch | **FIXED in V4-54**: a reply that finishes going out is followed by serving what is already buffered (`serve_buffered`), and a read takes at most the input buffer's room (`conn_io_in_room`). Tests: `test_authd_evloop` `test_pipelined` (both shapes, with a probe that the second frame was already buffered) and `test_backpressure`; campaign v83 BP5 and BP6 undo each half and are killed by name | V4-54 |

On macOS the 8192-byte Unix socket short-wrote, ASan checked only what was sent, the call timed out and the named check failed: KILLED(1 named) in V4-52. On Linux the socket took all of it and ASan aborted in `send` (`stack-buffer-overflow`, `localcli.c:121`) before the named check printed: SURVIVED(BAD) in the V4-50..53 control nightly (39646314151, artifact `mutations-v82`, `LC1.test`). The mutant was caught everywhere; what failed was the claim that the named check catches it. | v4 (V4-52, found by the V4-50..53 bring-up) | Info | `tools/mutations/mutate_v82.py` LC1; `tests/test_authd_cli.c` `test_localcli_robustness` | **FIXED in V4-55**: LC1 is the cap applied one byte late (`> AUTHD_LINE_MAX + 1u`), a mutant that fits the buffer. The old 8197-byte test passed against the re-authored LC1: the test, not only the mutation, had to change | V4-55 |
| F129 | **A peer that vanished with a reply still unsent kept its slot, and the event loop spun until the slot's deadline.** Found in V4-56 by a probe of what `poll` reports for a writer whose peer has gone: on macOS, a Unix socket and a loopback TCP socket alike report POLLHUP alone, with no POLLOUT and no POLLERR, and the next write is EPIPE. The loop wrote only on POLLOUT, and closed on POLLHUP only when nothing was pending (so that a peer that half-closes still gets its reply). So the slot stayed, and `poll` returned at once on every turn: 200 turns in 0 ms against a 10 ms timeout. It stopped at the slot's idle deadline. Bounded, and CPU only: other slots were still served. **Linux was never affected:** CI with the fix reverted (scratch branch `v4-56-ctl`, run 39687407344) failed this check on all three macOS jobs and passed it on every Linux job. Linux reports such a socket with POLLOUT or POLLERR, and the old loop already closed it. macOS is the development platform, not the deployment one. | v4 (V4-8a's event loop, found in V4-56) | Low | `apps/authd/evloop.c`, the POLLOUT dispatch | **FIXED in V4-56**: a hang-up with a reply pending is a write attempt. A peer that has gone answers EPIPE and the slot closes; one that only half-closed still gets its reply. Tests: `test_backpressure` 4 (the vanished peer is freed within 3 turns) and 5 (a half-closing peer gets the whole reply). Hand controls: the old loop fails 4; closing on every hang-up fails 5 | V4-56 |

The e2e leg that plants a regular file at the admin path removed it itself afterwards with `rm -f`, so it never saw the daemon do so. Only someone who can run the daemon with its configuration can trigger it. | v4 (V4-8a's listener, found in V4-57) | Med | `apps/authd/listener.c` `listener_close`; `authd_main.c` `listener_failed` | **FIXED in V4-57**: `listener_close` does nothing at all, path included, unless it holds a listener (a listener that fails after `bind()` already unlinks its own path). | F135 | **An enrolled handle's ServerHello comes about half a microsecond later than a decoy's.** §7.3's decoy flow gives a ClientHello for an unknown, revoked, superseded or disabled handle the same outcome as one for an enrolled handle; the time differs. Found by V4-65's `bench_decoy` over loopback. At 100,000 round trips a class, three runs gave max \|t\| 5.40, 6.05 and 4.92 (dudect's threshold is 4.5), the enrolled handle slower each time, medians 0.4–0.6 µs apart. At 4,000 a class no run shows it (35 saved runs, largest \|t\| 3.89). The cause is the store: in-process, `store_lookup_active` takes 4.79 µs on a hit and 4.50 µs on a miss plus the decoy key's copy (\|t\| 318), because a hit runs the three-table join and copies the key and user id, and a miss ends at its first index probe (`one_active_key`, which holds only active keys, so a revoked handle misses there too). Revoked and unknown handles do not differ, on the wire (max \|t\| 0.59 and 2.79 at 100,000) or in the store. What it gives a prober: whether a handle it already holds is active. It cannot find handles: they are 128 random bits (§3.1). The cost: one 100,000-a-class run is 200,000 handshakes, about four weeks from one address at the default `rate_per_min` of 5 (§7.2, §17), on loopback with no network jitter | v4 (§7.3's flow, measured in V4-65) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` `on_client_hello`; `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_lookup_active` | **FIXED in V4-67**, the owner's choice of a time floor: the pin's resolution does not return until 50 µs after the lookup began, whichever key it pinned (spec §7.3, erratum 53). It only waits; what is pinned and every byte after it are unchanged. Measured with it, at 100,000 a class: real vs unknown max \|t\| 2.13 and 1.80 end to end, medians 217.8/218.0 and 218.4/218.5 µs. In-process the floored step still differs by about 1 ns (\|t\| 3.67 and 8.83), from where the wait loop's clock reads end; that does not show end to end. Not hidden: a lookup slower than the floor, about 1 in 10,000 here. 10 µs was the first value; a Debug ASan lookup takes 14–15 µs, so it was raised to 50 µs. Test: `test_authd_conn` (13 floor checks, a stepped clock among them); campaign v87, FL1–FL6 | V4-65, V4-67 |
| F136 | **One malformed request ended the reference site's process.** `examples/site-node/server.mjs` called each API route without `await` inside its handler's `try`, so a route's rejection escaped the `catch`, and Node ends a process on an unhandled rejection. A POST whose body was not JSON, was a JSON `null`, or was over 16 KiB did it, and so did a WebSocket upgrade whose target the URL parser refuses, parsed outside any `catch`. A restart also loses the in-memory session table. Found by the defensive review that followed V4-65 and reproduced locally; behind the runbook's Caddy, the body cases reach the site. | v4 (V4-13c's reference site, found in V4-66's review) | Med | `examples/site-node/server.mjs`, the request handler, `body()`, the `upgrade` handler | **FIXED in V4-66**: the route is awaited; `body()` accepts only a JSON object (400 otherwise) and answers 413 over 16 KiB once the body is read, dropping the connection past 1 MiB; the `upgrade` handler closes a target it cannot parse, or any upgrade when no daemon address was given. Test: `site_sessions` (8 new checks, with the site in a child process); hand controls HC1–HC4 | V4-66 |
| F137 | **The threat model credited browser users with protection they do not have, and the browser client did not use its pin.** Two rows said an active attacker inside TLS, or a compromised Caddy, cannot impersonate the site, because the client checks a pinned server key. That holds for the CLI. A browser gets the page's code over the same TLS on every visit, so whoever controls that TLS decides what runs where the key is unlocked. And the client stored a server key at enrollment, as spec §14 says, but logged in and rotated with whatever key the page offered. Found answering the owner's question whether the system is secure end to end. | v4 (V4-1's threat model; V4-13c's browser client) | Med | `docs/v4/threat-model.md`; `web/client/flows.mjs` `login`, `rotate`; spec §14 | **FIXED in V4-68**, the owner's go: a login and a rotation use the pinned key and refuse a page that offers another (`PinMismatch`) before anything is sent. The threat model now separates the CLI and the browser in those rows, the trust boundaries and the residual risks. Spec §14 states what the pin cannot do (erratum 54), and the runbook tells operators to check the TLS key exchange is post-quantum. What remains is inherent, not fixed: a browser's protection against whoever controls the site's TLS is TLS's. Test: `web_flows` (3 checks); hand controls HC1–HC4 | V4-68 |
| F138 | **A handshake that never completed kept its slot past `handshake_timeout_ms`.** A protocol slot gets the handshake deadline (10 s) at accept, but `serve_buffered` re-armed every slot to the idle deadline (60 s) after serving any frame. So once the ClientHello was served the handshake deadline no longer applied, and each further frame that left the handshake unfinished (a malformed ClientAuth is retryable and uncounted, `authd_conn.c` `on_client_auth`) re-armed it again. A ClientHello needs no account: an unknown handle gets the decoy. What it gives: slots held for as long as the peer keeps sending, against `max_slots` (256) and `max_conns_per_addr` (8), so the number of addresses it would take to hold every slot is `max_slots` ÷ 8. Found reading the event loop in V4-69's adversarial review; the deadline rule is shown by the unit tests below, the exhaustion itself was not measured. | v4 (V4-8a's event loop) | Med | `apps/authd/evloop.c` `serve_buffered`; `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-69**, the owner's go: a protocol slot is re-armed to the idle deadline only once its handshake has completed (`authd_slot_t.established`, set where the login code is issued and cleared on release); until then the deadline set at accept stands. Local-API slots are unchanged. Tests: `test_authd_evloop` (a frame served before the handshake completes leaves the handshake deadline; the frame that completes it switches to idle; a released slot is not established), `test_authd_conn` (a completed login is established, on the idle deadline). Hand controls: the re-arm made unconditional fails the first; the marker removed fails the second | V4-69 |
| F139 | **A local-API connection closing reset the login in the protocol slot with the same index.** `evloop_close_slot` calls `on_close` for both pools, and `authd_conn_on_close`'s `conn_of` mapped any slot to `app->conns[slot->index]` without checking its kind. The local pool numbers its slots from 0 too, so a site.sock or admin.sock connection closing wiped the session or handshake of whichever login held the protocol slot with that index, and that connection went back to waiting for a ClientHello. The reference site opens and closes a site.sock connection per request and both pools hand out the lowest free slot first, so ordinary site traffic made concurrent logins fail at random. It also left the reset slot `established` (F138's marker), so a new handshake on it got the idle deadline. Found by V4-69's adversarial review (the parsing lane), reproduced against the ASan daemon: a second signed ServerHello on one TCP connection after one site.sock PING, and 3 of 8 real `authd_client` logins failed while site.sock traffic ran, none in the control. | v4 (V4-9a's local API beside V4-8b's connections) | Med | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` `conn_of`, `authd_conn_on_close`; `apps/authd/evloop.c` `evloop_close_slot` | **FIXED in V4-70**: `conn_of` returns a connection only for a protocol slot. Test: `test_authd_localapi` (a login waiting for its ClientAuth survives a site.sock connection on the local slot with its index being served and closed, and completes). Hand control: the kind check removed fails it | V4-70 |

No memory effect: the input buffer is fixed (12,317 bytes) and lines are parsed in place. Found by V4-69's review (parsing lane), reproduced: 12,000 bytes in three writes was served, 9,000 in one was refused. | v4 (V4-9a) | Low | `apps/authd/conn_io.c` `note_line_cap` | **FIXED in V4-72**: every line in the buffer, complete or not, is measured against the cap. Tests: `test_authd_evloop` (8192 bytes with its LF in three reads is served; 8193 is refused; an over-cap line behind a complete one is refused). Hand control: the old check fails the last two | V4-72 |

Within one line only (newlines were escaped); it could mislead a parser that keeps the last key or a grep-based alert. Not under `log_identities = hashed`, which the example config sets. Spec §3 and §15 also named two different escapes. | F142 | **The threat model said a compromised Caddy cannot read the protocol; the handshake's identities are cleartext.** Spec-v2 §6.3.1 puts the initiator's id (the device handle, in the daemon flow) and the responder's id in the clear in ClientHello and ServerHello, and whether a record follows ClientAuth shows whether the login succeeded. Whoever sees the protocol's bytes -- a compromised Caddy, anything on the proxy→daemon socket, or a recorder who later breaks a classical TLS key exchange -- learns each logging-in device's handle and outcome, and for that attacker the decoy flow and F135's floor hide nothing. No gain in session content or in authenticating. | F143 | **`/api/recover` tells anyone whether a username exists, how many recovery codes it has, and its role, and lets anyone lock its recovery.** The route needs no session and the reference site has no rate limit. Measured: no such user 0.0 ms; 0 codes 0.1 ms; 5 codes 255 ms; 16 codes 796 ms -- one request, no statistics. Five requests, malformed ones included, lock that user's recovery for an hour. Still possible: anyone can spend a user's five attempts and lock its recovery for an hour, now at the route's per-client rate. Tests: `test_authd_localapi` (eight kinds of refusal each cost exactly one; a legacy code still recovers), `site_sessions` (one reply; 429 past the burst). The threat model, spec §14 and `enroll.html` say XSS gets the blob for offline guessing, with Argon2id and the passphrase policy as the defence; with the passphrase in the field neither applies. The code is guarded by `test_handshake`. | F155 | **V4-76's F143 fix removed the bound on the hashing anonymous requests could cause.** Before V4-76 an unknown or locked user was refused at once, so recovery's Argon2id work was bounded by five attempts per real user an hour. V4-76 made every refusal cost one computation (~43 ms), and a made-up username never locks, so the only limit left was the reference site's per-client bucket, keyed on the full address: one IPv6 /64 is an endless supply of fresh buckets. Measured in process by round 2's review: a site.sock PING (0.03 ms) waited 149 ms behind 30 refusals for usernames that do not exist on three connections; the stall per turn is about the number of busy site.sock connections × 43 ms (8 allowed by default). Introduced by V4-76 and found by the round-2 review (parsing lane), reproduced. | v4 (V4-76) | Med | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_recovery_use`; `examples/site-node/server.mjs` the recovery limiter | **FIXED in V4-78**, within the owner's go for F143: `RECOVERY-USE` on site.sock takes a daemon-wide budget (4 at once, 2 a second; `RECOVERY_USE_BURST`, `RECOVERY_USE_PER_SEC`, assigned by the daemon and pinned by `check_spec_constants.sh`) before any lookup or hashing, and answers `busy` past it, at no cost and the same whoever is named; admin.sock is not counted. The site keys an IPv6 client by its /64 and an IPv4-mapped one by its IPv4. Erratum 59. The cost: a sustained stream of refusals can keep real users' recovery `busy`, which a per-user lockout could not prevent either. Round 2 measured, in process at the spec's cost, 500 samples a class interleaved: unknown 43,273 µs, active with 16 codes 43,650 µs, no codes 43,600 µs, locked 43,416 µs (Mann-Whitney z −5.9 to −2.9); at the KDF's minimum, 21 µs unknown, 23 µs locked, ~65 µs active. On macOS; Linux's fsync was not measured and would likely widen it. Exploiting it needs many samples per user, against five attempts an hour per user, the site's per-client limit, F155's budget and a network's jitter. V4-76's code comment and decision had claimed the time was uniform. | F157 | **A login code issued before a revocation still exchanged for a token after it — an account takeover after recovery.** `EXCHANGE` never asked whether the code's device or user was still active. A thief with a stolen device key can always hold a fresh code (a successful login refunds its rate-limit token, F81). Reproduced at the daemon by round 2: the owner runs `RECOVERY-USE revoke=all` and enrolls a replacement; the thief exchanges the held code (`OK token=`), mints new recovery codes (superseding the owner's), recovers with `revoke=all` and enrolls its own device; at the end the owner's replacement cannot log in and the thief's device logs in as the user. The same code held across `DISABLE-USER` exchanged for a 12-hour token that worked again after `ENABLE-USER`. Found by the round-2 review (logic lane), reproduced. | v4 (V4-8b's login code, V4-9's revocation) | **High** | `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_consume_login_code_ex`; `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_exchange` | **FIXED in V4-79, on a branch; landing on main awaits the owner's go** (a High): `EXCHANGE` reads the device's and the user's status in the transaction that spends the code, and refuses `device-revoked` or `user-disabled`, spending the code so a re-enable cannot revive it (erratum 60). The reference site's `/api/recovery-codes`, which relies on that `EXCHANGE`, is closed by the same check. Tests: `test_authd_store` (four checks), `test_authd_localapi` (a code from before `REVOKE-DEVICE`, and from before `DISABLE-USER` then `ENABLE-USER`). | F175 | **CI turned red whenever GitHub's release CDN did.** Every job downloaded libsodium from GitHub's releases, liboqs from GitHub and SQLite from sqlite.org on every run, with no cache and no second source. On 2026-11-08 the CDN answered 503 and 504 for hours: three of main's CI jobs and three nightly attempts failed with nothing wrong in the code. | v4 (V3's CI) | Low | `.github/workflows/*.yml`; `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`; `deploy/fetch-deps.sh` | **FIXED in V4-85**, the owner's go: a composite action (`.github/actions/deps`) restores `deploy/fetch-deps.sh`'s directory from the Actions cache, keyed on the files holding the pins, and on a miss fetches it with five retries; the action sets `MLDSA_DEPS_CACHE` to `$RUNNER_TEMP/mldsa-deps` for the rest of the job, and `Dependencies.cmake` now also reads it from the environment. (V4-85's first version put the cache under `${{ github.workspace }}/..`; actions/cache rejects a path with `..` with only a warning at the post-job save, so nothing was ever saved and every job fetched. Found reading the first nightly's logs; fixed in V4-85c.) libsodium has a second source, its own mirror, which served a byte-identical file. Every artifact is still checked against the same pins at configure time, so neither the cache nor the mirror is trusted more than a fresh fetch. Shown: a fresh tree configured with only the environment variable took all three from the cache, built, and passed 51/53; with GitHub's URL broken on purpose, `fetch-deps.sh` fell back to the mirror and the pin held | V4-85 |

The tables give the passphrase's length and where its separators and listed words fall, and stayed in the client's memory (the CLI, the browser's wasm module) after the call. Found by the V4-86 review of V4-85's code, by reading. | v4 (V4-85) | Info | `apps/authd/passphrase.c` `word_estimate` | **FIXED in V4-87**: the tables are on the stack and wiped with the rest. | F177 | **The reference site's Origin checks accepted a DNS-rebinding page.** Both the POST check (V4-13c) and the WebSocket check (V4-84, F160) compare `Origin` with the request's own `Host`. The page then drives the loopback site and, through `/authd/v1`, the daemon from the visitor's browser -- without the visitor's cookies, which belong to the real name. Behind Caddy nothing changes: Caddy serves only the site's own name. Reproduced against the test daemon: an anonymous request added a device to an existing account, which then logged in as that account. The daemon behaved as specified; the site is its trusted enroller (spec §4). Nothing had ever started the unit as written. Found by the first rehearsal of `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` on a systemd host (V4-99, `tools/rehearse_runbook.py`, CI run 42769079909 and its successors). | v4 (the runbook, V4-13c) | Low | `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` steps 4 and 14 | **FIXED in V4-99**: the site gets its own world-readable copy of `server.pub` (it is public) and its own 0700 directory for the invite, and `www-data` joins the daemon's group; the rehearsal proves the site's EXCHANGE reaches the daemon by the daemon's own `event=local-request cmd=EXCHANGE uid=33` line | V4-99 |
| F183 | **The daemon refused the credential systemd delivers.** `authd_secret_read` required the passphrase file to be owned by the daemon's euid with no group or other bit, and the unit, the header and the runbook all said systemd delivers "a 0400 file owned by the service user". Measured on Ubuntu 26.04: root:root, mode 0440, read through an ACL entry for the service user (the group bits are the ACL mask), on a private read-only tmpfs. So the deployment path the custody rule was written for could never start: `passphrase file …: not-owned-by-this-user`, exit 3. Found by the first rehearsal of `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` on a systemd host (V4-99, `tools/rehearse_runbook.py`, CI run 42769079909 and its successors). | v4 (V4-9b, V4-11) | Medium | `apps/authd/authd_secret.c` (custody); `deploy/mldsa-authd.service`; spec §12 | **FIXED in V4-99**: a file directly in `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY` may be root:root with no group write and no other bit; every other file keeps the old rule (erratum 74). The decision is a pure function, `authd_secret_custody`, with 16 unit checks; v79 gains PF8–PF14 and PF6 now dies on macOS too. The rehearsal is the end-to-end proof: real systemd, real credential, the daemon starts | V4-99 |

| F187 | **A configuration the daemon cannot open is reported as `io`.** `authd_config_load` maps every `open`/`fstat`/`read` failure to one status, so a permissions error (F180) and a missing file read the same: `authd.conf: io`. Three new checks; two controls red by name. The errno preserved across a failing `fstat` is defensive and no test reaches it (a third control survived, as expected) | V4-101 |
| F188 | **A restore after a crash did not restore.** The runbook said "a restore is a file copy: stop the service, replace `store.sqlite3`, start it". The store runs in SQLite's WAL mode, and after a crash, the usual reason to restore, the latest transactions live in `store.sqlite3-wal`; left beside the restored file, that log is replayed into it when the daemon opens it. Reproduced on the deployed stack (V4-100): a user enrolled from a browser after step 12's backup, the daemon killed with SIGKILL (a 136 KB `-wal` left behind), the store replaced as the runbook said, the daemon started, and `list-users` still listed that user. A restore meant to undo something, a device enrolled by someone else, would have kept it. | v4 (the runbook, V4-11) | Medium | `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` step 12 | **FIXED in V4-100**: the restore is now a command block that removes `store.sqlite3-wal` and `-shm` before installing the backup, and the rehearsal runs that block after a real crash and requires the post-backup user gone and the audit chain verifying; a control without the `rm` fails there | V4-100 |
| F189 | **The runbook quoted one CMake's wording as what `ctest` must print.** Step 13 said `ctest` "must report `100% tests passed out of 3`"; CMake 3.28, Ubuntu 24.04's, prints `100% tests passed, 0 tests failed out of 3` for the same result, so an operator there sees something the runbook does not promise. Found when the rehearsal first ran on a second Ubuntu (V4-103). | v4 (the runbook, V4-13b) | Info | `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` step 13 | **FIXED in V4-103**: both wordings are given; the rehearsal accepts either and runs on both Ubuntus | V4-103 |
| F190 | **Changing the server passphrase breaks the audit chain and every pseudonym.** `key_audit` (spec §9.2) and `key_log` (§15) are HKDF of the envelope KEK, which is Argon2id of the server passphrase; `authd_admin rewrap` re-seals the key under a new passphrase and therefore a new KEK. Reproduced (V4-104): a store with 2 audit entries verified under the old passphrase, and after `rewrap` the same untouched store failed `audit-verify` as `audit-chain-corrupt` after 2 entries; `pseudonym --id alice` changed from `c501b14b69f695c7` to `abea4964d1e261de`. A permanent false tamper alarm, after an operation the tool offers, teaches an operator to ignore the alarm. | v4 (V4-9b's key hierarchy) | Medium | spec §9.2, §15; `apps/authd/authd_cli.c` (`cmd_rewrap`) | **Fixed in V4-108** (the owner chose the sealed root key; spec erratum 79, `MLDSAEK1` version 2). The server key file now seals a random 32-byte store root key after the image, `key_audit` and `key_log` are HKDF of that root, and `rewrap` reseals the same root under the new passphrase. A version-1 file's KEK stands in for the root, so a store made before V4-108 keeps verifying with nothing done to it, and its first `rewrap` writes version 2 with that KEK as the root. `test_authd_cli`: a store with 2 audit entries verifies after `rewrap` and after a second one, the root key and `alice`'s pseudonym are unchanged, and a key with a different root fails `audit-verify` (the control); the same from a version-1 file. Campaign v88 (11 mutations) kills each piece by name, RK1 being the original defect. The runbook's step 23 is the procedure, rehearsed in CI. V4-104's warning is gone with its cause. Not changed: a copy of the old file still opens under the old passphrase, and a daemon older than V4-108 refuses a version-2 file (spec §18) | V4-108 |

A check that passes on the wrong file is worse than one that fails. | v4 (V4-9c, V4-13d) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_cli.c` `cmd_audit_verify`, `cmd_pseudonym` | **FIXED in V4-109**: both use `store_open_existing`: no `SQLITE_OPEN_CREATE`, a file with no `meta` table refused before the schema runs, a database with no store id refused instead of given one. `test_authd_store` (six checks: `io`, nothing created; an empty file `store-corrupt` and still empty; no store id `store-corrupt` and still none; a real store opens with the same pseudonym) and `test_authd_cli` (both commands exit 1 as `io` and create nothing). | F202 | **An IPv4 client that a proxy reports as IPv4-mapped IPv6 shares one rate-limit bucket with every other such client.** The PROXY v2 parser kept an `AF_INET6` source as IPv6 whatever it was, and an IPv6 address is limited by its /64. `::ffff:a.b.c.d` has 64 zero bits first, so `::ffff:203.0.113.7` and `::ffff:198.51.100.9` had the same key: all IPv4 clients behind such a proxy would share 5 connections a minute and 8 at once, and `log_client_ip = prefix` would log each as `::/48`. Reproduced at the parser (two mapped sources, equal keys). **Not reproduced through a proxy:** Caddy, the proxy the runbook deploys, sends an IPv4 client as `AF_INET` (`caddy_proxy` asserts `src=127.0.0.1`); a proxy that listens on one dual-stack socket and copies the kernel's address would send the mapped form, and none was measured. | F203 | **A passphrase prompt that is stopped and continued shows the rest of the passphrase.** `authd_client --passphrase-prompt` turned echo off and caught `SIGINT`, `SIGTERM`, `SIGHUP` and `SIGQUIT`, but not the stop signals. Whatever was typed next appeared on screen, and so in scrollback and in any session recording. Measured on a pty under bash 3.2.57 and zsh 5.9 (macOS): in all six scenarios of `tools/prompt_job_control.py` (Ctrl-Z then `fg`; started with `&` then `fg`; Ctrl-Z, `bg`, `fg`) the passphrase typed afterwards was echoed; in the two started in the background the client had exited `not-a-terminal` and the line went to the shell as a command. Needs the person to stop the prompt themselves, and someone or something that sees the screen. No Linux shell was measured. | v4 (V4-13d) | Low | `apps/authd/cli_prompt.c` `cli_prompt_read()` | **FIXED in V4-115**: `SIGTSTP`, `SIGTTIN` and `SIGTTOU` are caught too; the terminal is restored before the process stops, and on the continue the prompt reads the terminal's settings again, turns echo off, discards what was typed before the stop and asks again (spec §13, erratum 84). An ignored `SIGTSTP` stays ignored. The same six scenarios: nothing echoed, key written. Eleven new checks, seven named for F203, with a handler standing in for the shell; campaign v94 | V4-115 |
| F204 | **A second daemon on a configuration with no TCP listener took the running daemon's sockets.** `listener_open_unix` unlinked any socket at its path and bound a new one, on the assumption that an existing socket is a leftover from a crash. F130's fix (V4-57) stopped a daemon that FAILED to start from unlinking paths it did not hold, and its test passes because a second daemon fails at the TCP bind before it reaches a Unix socket. With `listen_port = 0`, which `deploy/authd.conf.example` offers ("Leave it at 0 to disable it entirely"), nothing fails. Reproduced with the built binaries: the second daemon logged `started`; the admin socket's inode changed; the first daemon stayed up, held three sockets nobody could reach, and logged nothing. When the second daemon was stopped it unlinked the paths, and `list-users` answered `cannot-connect` with the first daemon still running. Not remotely reachable: it needs someone who can start the daemon with its configuration (a hand-run while debugging, an overlapping restart, a second unit on the same file). | v4 (V4-9a) | Medium | `apps/authd/listener.c` `listener_open_unix()` | **FIXED in V4-118**: before unlinking, the daemon connects to the socket. Only a refused connection (or a path that vanished) makes it a leftover; a socket that answers, or that the daemon cannot judge, is refused with the new status `in-use` and left alone (spec §16, erratum 85). Not a lock: two daemons started at the same instant over a leftover can both find it dead. Two unit checks and an end-to-end leg, all shown failing before the fix; a killed daemon's sockets are still replaced (unit and end-to-end canaries); campaign v95 | V4-118 |
| F205 | **A daemon with fewer open files allowed than it has slots stopped answering on every socket, and said nothing.** Each slot is an open descriptor, `max_slots` goes to 4096, and nothing compared it to `RLIMIT_NOFILE`; `accept_ready` returned on any `accept()` error without logging or counting it. Reproduced with the built daemon before the fix: `ulimit -n 64`, the default `max_slots = 256`, 75 idle connections opened and held. 54 were accepted. After that no listener accepted anything: `authd_admin list-users` on the admin socket answered `malformed-response`, with a hint about the uid allowlist, and the log had no line about any of it. On macOS the process slept in `poll`, because macOS drops the connection `accept()` could not take; Linux leaves it queued and the listener readable, so there the loop does not sleep (reasoned from accept(2), not measured locally). The defaults are not affected under systemd (296 needed, soft limit 1024). A `max_slots` of about 1000 or more is, and `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` step 8 tabulates 1024, 2048 and 4096 with the `LimitMEMLOCK` each needs and said nothing about open files; the shipped unit set no `LimitNOFILE`. Reachable from the network once the configuration is in that state: connections held open are all it takes, and the slots that would have refused them with a log line are never reached. | v4 (V4-9a) | Medium | `apps/authd/authd_main.c`; `apps/authd/evloop.c` `accept_ready()`; `apps/authd/listener.c` `listener_accept_ex()` | **FIXED in V4-120**: at startup the daemon computes `max_slots + max_local_slots + 32`, raises its own soft limit to it when the hard limit allows (`fd-limit` at INFO), and when it does not, logs `fd-limit` at WARN and says so on standard error with the numbers (`apps/authd/fdlimit.c`). A failed `accept()` is counted, logged as `accept-failed` at WARN once per run of failures, and that listener is not polled for 100 ms; `ECONNABORTED` is retried like `EINTR` (spec §15, §16, erratum 86). What is left: under a hard limit that is short, the admin socket is still locked out when the descriptors run out, now with two log lines saying why. A descriptor reserved for the admin socket was not built. Unit checks in `test_authd_evloop` for the arithmetic, the raise, the short hard limit (in a child process) and the accept path under a real `RLIMIT_NOFILE`, with canaries that accepting resumes; two end-to-end legs on the built daemon; campaign v96 | V4-120 |
| F206 | **A SIGHUP killed the daemon, and systemd did not start it again.** `authd_main.c` installed handlers for `SIGTERM` and `SIGINT` only. `SIGHUP`, the signal an operator sends a daemon to make it re-read its configuration, kept its default action: the process died with no drain, no `draining` or `stopped` line, and its sockets left on disk. systemd counts termination by `SIGHUP` as a clean exit, so the unit's `Restart=on-failure` did not apply and the service stayed stopped. Reproduced with the built daemon (`kill -HUP`: gone, the log ends at `listening-unix`) and measured on the deployed service in CI run 43154418299: after `systemctl kill -s HUP mldsa-authd` the unit was `ActiveState=inactive`, `SubState=dead`, `Result=success`, `NRestarts=0`, on Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04 alike. Ignored and not turned into a drain, because a drained daemon is the same outage. Not a reload: there is none, and the unit, the runbook and the spec now say so. What changes for a hand-run daemon: it outlives the terminal it was started in. An end-to-end leg on the built daemon, shown failing first (died), with the canary that `SIGTERM` still drains and stops it afterwards; a step in CI's deployment rehearsal that sends the signal through systemd and requires the same main PID, `active`, and the log line; campaign v97 | V4-121 |

The local slots keep their descriptors. The end-to-end leg, shown failing first: 70 connections held against a limit of 64, exactly 24 accepted, no `accept-failed`, and `authd_admin` answers. | F210 | **The reference site logged a user out whenever it could not reach the daemon.** `/api/me` treated every failure of `VERIFY` as the daemon refusing the token: it deleted the session, cleared the cookie and answered 401. A daemon that is restarting, a socket that is not there yet, a connection closed mid-answer and an `internal` answer are none of them a refusal, and the token is still good afterwards: tokens are in the store, and one issued before a restart verifies after it (measured on the real daemon in `authd_e2e`). So every daemon restart, upgrade or watchdog kill (F209) logged out every user who loaded a page during it. The same catch answered with the error's `code`, which for a socket failure is Node's own (`401 {"error":"ENOENT"}`, and `403 {"error":"ENOENT"}` from enroll), and login and recovery told a user their login or recovery code had failed when nothing had been asked. `withAuthd` also had no time limit, so a daemon that accepted and never answered held the browser's request open for as long as it hung. Found by asking what the site does during the restart F209 now causes on purpose. The shipped command line refuses to start without `--invite-file`, so only a site embedding `startSite` was open. Found by reading every comparison of a secret in the site | v4 | Low | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-129**: the offered invite must be a string and is compared as two SHA-256 digests with `timingSafeEqual`; `startSite` throws unless the invite is a non-empty string. 15 named `invite:` checks in `site_sessions` (ten wrong offers of every type refused alike, the canary, three sites that must not start, and no direct comparison left in the source); 6 hand mutations, 6 killed by name | V4-129 |
| F213 | **One unauthenticated client ended the reference site's process, when the site is reached without a proxy in front.** The site refuses a WebSocket upgrade from another origin or under another Host (F160, F177) by writing `403` to the socket. From the `upgrade` event on, that socket is the handler's, and the refusal branch attached no `'error'` listener: a client that read the 403 and then reset the connection (`RST`) raised `ECONNRESET` on a socket nobody listened to, which Node turns into an uncaught exception. Measured in a child process: the site exited, and every session went with it. The same branch only half-closed the socket (`end()`), so a client that kept its own half open held it for as long as it liked (measured: still open 4 s after the 403, with nothing in the site to end it). Behind Caddy, as deployed, `/authd/v1` goes to the daemon and never reaches this handler, and every other upgrade path is destroyed outright; a site exposed directly (development, the phone timing tool, an embedding of `startSite`) was open. Found by an independent read of the site's JavaScript, then reproduced | v4 | Medium | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-130**: the handler listens for `'error'` on the socket before anything else, and a refused upgrade's socket is destroyed one second after the refusal is written. 3 named `malformed:` checks in `site_sessions`, against the site in a child process (a refusal for each reason followed by a reset, and a client that holds its half open); 3 hand mutations, 3 killed by name | V4-130 |
| F214 | **A malformed login code was answered `503 unavailable`, as if the daemon were away.** V4-127 (F210) made every failure that is not one of the daemon's refusals a 503. The site converts the browser's base64url code to hex before asking the daemon, and a code that is not 32 bytes made that conversion throw a plain `Error`, which the new rule read as an outage. `POST /api/login` with `{"code":"x"}` answered 503 (measured, as did the empty string, 31 bytes, 33 bytes and a hex string), and so did the second login on `/api/recovery-codes`. Before V4-127 it was `403 login-failed`. Nothing was disclosed and no session was lost; a wrong answer told a client, or a monitor counting 503s, that the daemon was down when it was not. A regression introduced by V4-127's fix | v4 | Low | `examples/site-node/authd.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-130**: the conversion throws `AuthdError('bad-request-field')`, the class this module uses for every other value it refuses to send, so the site answers `403 login-failed`. 8 named `code:` checks in `site_sessions` (five malformed codes, the recovery-codes route, the session kept, and the canary: a well-formed unknown code); 1 hand mutation, killed by name | V4-130 |
| F215 | **A flood of requests with no cookie evicted a visitor in the middle of a recovery, and their single-use recovery code was already spent.** The site caps the sessions nobody has logged into and evicts the least recently used (F132), on the reasoning that such a visitor simply starts the login again. Two kinds of session are not logged in and cannot simply start again: one holding a recovery ticket, whose code the daemon has already consumed, and one holding a signup the site has vouched for. A request without a cookie makes a new session and costs its sender nothing. Measured with the cap at 50: after 1,000 cookieless requests both sessions were gone (`recovering=false`, a new cookie issued). At the default cap of 10,000 the independent reviewer measured 540 ms for the flood on loopback. A flood kept up through a visitor's recoveries would spend all five of their codes with no device enrolled. Found by an independent read of the site's JavaScript, then reproduced | v4 | Medium | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-132**: eviction runs in three passes over the sessions not logged in: those holding nothing, then vouched signups (the invite is shared, so its holder can make any number), then tickets (each cost a valid recovery code). The cap is unchanged and still bounds every kind. 7 named `bound:` checks in `site_sessions`; 6 hand mutations, 6 killed by name. Not changed: a session idle for 30 minutes still expires, ticket or not | V4-132 |
| F216 | **A link on any other site to one of the site's API routes logged the visitor out.** The session cookie is `SameSite=Strict`, so a browser leaves it out of a navigation that another site started. Measured in headless Chromium with a logged-in visitor: a page on `localhost` linking to `http://127.0.0.1:…/api/me` or `/api/session`, one click, and the cookie was replaced and `/api/me` answered 401. An `<img>` pointing at the same routes did not do it: the browser discards a Strict cookie set by a cross-site subresource. Nothing is disclosed and the daemon's token is not revoked, only orphaned until it expires; the cost is that any page, mail or chat message can end a visitor's session, a recovery in progress included, whose code is then spent. Found by an independent read of the site's JavaScript; the browser half was unproven until this step | v4 | Medium | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-133**: an API request whose `Sec-Fetch-Site` is present and is neither `same-origin` nor `none` is answered `403 cross-site request refused` before any session is looked up or made; pages and static files are served as before, so links to the site still work. 11 named `nav:` checks in `site_sessions` (7 red before the fix) and 2 `cross-site:` checks in `browser_e2e` with a real cross-site link (both red before); 7 hand mutations, 7 killed by name. | F217 | **The journal scans could not see a logged secret that holds a space or `=`.** The tests that search a captured journal for secrets (`tests/journal_scan.h`, CLAIMS D13) rebuild how the daemon writes an `id=` field and look for 16-character windows of it. V4-72 (F141) changed the daemon to print a space and `=` as `.`; the scan's copy of the rule was not changed, so any window holding either byte never matched. A random 32-byte value logged as an identifier went unseen 16,182 times in 2,000,000 simulated draws (1 in 124). Found when campaign v71's mutant EI2 (the ClientHello nonce logged where the id was) survived in nightly 43269079927 on a commit that touched no C; the same job passed on rerun. Product code is not affected: the daemon logged nothing it should not; the tests' ability to notice was weaker than claimed | v4 | Low | `tests/journal_scan.h` | **FIXED in V4-134**: the scan's rule is the logger's, and `test_authd_log` compares the two over all 256 byte values and searches for a logged value built so that every window holds a space or `=`. 4 named `scan:` checks, 2 red before the fix. The simulated miss rate is now 16 in 500,000 (1 in 31,000): a value with fewer than two printable bytes in every window, which the scan skips by design | V4-134 |
| F218 | **Rotating a key in two tabs at once could leave the browser holding a key the daemon never saw.** The browser's store makes each step of a rotation one transaction, but a rotation is four steps with the daemon in the middle, and nothing kept two of them apart. Tab B, starting while tab A's `ROTATE` was unanswered, found A's pending key beside a current key that still logged in, which is exactly what a crashed rotation leaves; it discarded A's pending key as stale and staged its own. A's `ROTATE` then committed, and A's promote moved B's key over the current one. The daemon accepted A's new key, of which no copy was left; the browser held B's, which was never sent. Every later login failed, and the device needed a recovery code or a new enrollment. A login in a second tab between A's acknowledgement and its promote also completed the rotation underneath it, so a rotation that had succeeded reported an error. Found by a reviewer agent reading `flows.mjs` (candidate 4 of the V4-130 review); reproduced in `web_flows` against the real daemon before any change. | v4 (V4-13c's browser client) | Med | `web/client/flows.mjs` `rotate`, `resolveKey`; `web/client/store.mjs` `promote`; spec §10.2 | **FIXED in V4-136.** A rotation, and the resolving of a pending key, run only while holding a per-identity Web Lock that every tab of the origin shares (`store.exclusive`); the record is read again under the lock. `rotate` refuses where the lock cannot span tabs. `promote` takes the envelope its caller staged and moves no other. Spec v1.50, erratum 92. Not closed: a tab killed after its `ROTATE` left and before the daemon committed it releases the lock, and a rotation started in that instant can still discard the key about to be committed; the CLI has the same window for a process killed mid-rotation. Tests: `web_flows` (6 checks, 4 red before), `browser_e2e` (3 checks on real IndexedDB and Web Locks); hand mutations R1–R7, seven killed by name | V4-136 |
| F219 | **A daemon stopped while it was answering the site dropped the reply.** A handler queues its reply and the event loop writes it on its next turn. On `SIGTERM` the daemon's loop ended as soon as no *protocol* connection was left (`evloop_active`, which counts that pool alone), and the connections of the local API were then closed with whatever they still had queued. The time a local request takes is almost all inside its handler — one Argon2id computation for `RECOVERY-USE` and `RECOVERY-ISSUE`, a store transaction for `ENROLL` — so a stop that arrived during a request arrived, nearly always, before its reply was written. The effect was already in the store: a recovery code spent and the ticket it bought never delivered; a user's old recovery codes replaced and the new ones never shown; a login code exchanged and the session token lost; a device enrolled and the site answering 503. Every restart of the service (an upgrade, a reboot, `systemctl restart`) was a chance of it. `evloop.h` promised that a stop lets queued writes flush, and the daemon's loop did not ask the question that covers local connections. Found by a reviewer agent reading `authd_main.c` (candidate 1 of the V4-136 review); reproduced against the shipped daemon before any change: 8 of 8 stops sent 20 ms into a `RECOVERY-USE` lost its reply. | v4 (V4-8b's local API on V4-8a's drain) | Med | `apps/authd/authd_main.c` (the loop's exit); `apps/authd/evloop.c` `evloop_drained`; spec §16 | **FIXED in V4-137.** `evloop_drained()` is true only after a stop and with both pools empty, and the daemon's loop ends on it. A local connection with nothing queued is still closed on the next turn, and one whose peer will not read goes at its deadline, so the stop is bounded as before. Spec v1.51, erratum 93, defines "drain". Tests: `test_authd_evloop` (13 checks, 3 red before the fix), `authd_e2e` (a real daemon sent `SIGTERM` 20 ms into a `RECOVERY-USE`; attempts whose reply beat the signal are repeated, not counted); mutations v101 DR1–DR5, five killed by name | V4-137 |
| F220 | **A key the site refused to enroll stayed in the browser, and so did one from an attempt the site never answered.** The enrollment page makes a key, stores it in IndexedDB and then asks the site to enroll it; that order is right, because the other one can enroll a key that is then not stored. But the page did nothing about the answer. A refusal (the name is taken, the recovery ticket has expired) left the key in the store; so did a 503 or a lost connection, and a second attempt made a second key. The login page listed every stored key by its random handle, with nothing to say which the daemon knew, and a login with the wrong one fails as a login with a wrong passphrase does. Shown in headless Chromium: enrolling a taken name left two keys in the store, one of them never sent to the daemon. No key or account is lost and nobody gains access; a user is left choosing between handles, and a failed choice looks like a forgotten passphrase | v4 (V4-13c's browser client) | Low | `web/pages/enroll.mjs`; `web/pages/login.mjs`; `web/client/flows.mjs`; `web/client/store.mjs`; spec §14 | **FIXED in V4-138.** `flows.enroll()` stores the key marked `unconfirmed`, asks the site, and acts on the answer: a yes clears the mark; a refusal (the site's 4xx) removes the record, since the daemon never took the key; no answer (no connection, a 5xx) keeps it, still marked, because the daemon may have enrolled it and the answer been lost, and the page names the handle. The login page lists confirmed keys first and labels the others, and `flows.login()` clears the mark when the daemon accepts the key. Records written before this are confirmed ones. Spec v1.52, erratum 94. Tests: `web_flows` against the real daemon (9 checks: refused, enrolled, unanswered and not enrolled, unanswered and enrolled, policy), `browser_e2e` on real IndexedDB (4 checks, 1 red before the fix); ten hand mutations of `flows.mjs`, `store.mjs` and the pages, ten killed by name. No C changed. A key from an unanswered attempt that the daemon did not enroll stayed, labelled, until the user removed the site's data; since V4-140 the login page can forget such a key (spec erratum 95) | V4-138 |
| F221 | **The runbook rehearsal failed whenever it ran across midnight UTC.** Step 12 takes a backup named by `$(date +%F)`, and step 16 restores it. The harness filled the restore block's `YYYY-MM-DD` placeholder with a second `$(date +%F)`, evaluated minutes later. A rehearsal that took its backup at 23:59 and restored at 00:01 looked for tomorrow's file: `install: cannot stat '…/backups/2026-11-13.sqlite3'`, the store was not restored, and steps 16, 19, 20 and 21 failed with it. Seen once, on V4-140's branch CI (run 43332171050, ubuntu-26.04 job, backup directory dated Nov 12 23:59, restore at Nov 13 00:01); a change with no C and no runbook edit went red. The runbook itself is not wrong: it tells the operator to name the backup to restore. | v4 (V4-100's restore rehearsal) | Low | `tools/rehearse_runbook.py` (step 12's substitution) | **FIXED in V4-141.** The harness restores the newest file in the backup directory, which is the one the rehearsal just took on a fresh machine, whatever the date. The old and new expressions were run against a directory holding only yesterday's backup: the old one finds nothing, the new one finds the file. **Not closed:** the rehearsal cannot be made to cross midnight on demand, so the fix is shown by that local comparison and not by a red CI run. | V4-141 |
| F222 | **`VERIFY` answered `unknown` for a valid token when the store could not be written.** `VERIFY` calls the store twice: once to learn the token's role, once to slide its idle window by that role's interval. Both calls write the token's row. The status of the second was dropped (`(void)store_verify_token(...)`), and a call that fails leaves its verdict at "unknown", so a write that failed there (the store busy past the daemon's 250 ms wait, a full disk, an I/O error) produced `ERR code=unknown` for a token that was valid. That is a refusal, and since erratum 91 a refusal is the one answer on which a site ends the session: the person is logged out by a fault that `internal` would have turned into a 503 and a retry. The window is narrow, because the first call's write has to succeed and the second's fail. Found by a reviewer agent reading `localapi.c`; reproduced before any change with a trigger, planted from a second connection, that lets the first write through and refuses the one that slides. | v4 (V4-9a's `VERIFY`) | Low | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_verify`; spec §8 | **FIXED in V4-143.** The second call's status is checked and a failure answers `internal`. Spec v1.54, erratum 96, says that any request can answer `internal` and that it is not a verdict. `test_authd_localapi` `test_store_write_failure`: `internal` with the trigger, `OK` for the same token without it. Campaign v102, SW1–SW3. | V4-143 |
| F223 | **A wrong recovery code that could not be counted was still answered `invalid`.** `RECOVERY-USE` counts each wrong code against the user's lockout with one write, and dropped that write's status. When it failed, the daemon answered `invalid` as usual and the lockout had not seen the guess. Nobody outside the machine can make the store fail, so this is not a way round the lockout; but an answer of `invalid` promises that the attempt was counted, and for as long as the store refuses writes every guess was free. Found in the same review as F222 and reproduced the same way, with a trigger refusing the update of `recovery_fail_count`. | v4 (V4-10's recovery codes) | Low | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_recovery_use`; spec §8 | **FIXED in V4-143.** A failed count answers `internal`, which a site turns into a 503. Same test: `internal` with the trigger, `invalid` without it. Campaign v102, SW4 and SW5. **Not closed:** `EXCHANGE` drops the status of the read that looks up the user's role and falls back to a user's lifetimes; a read cannot be failed by a trigger, so there is no failing test for it yet and it is unchanged. | V4-143 |
| F224 | **A login that arrived while the daemon was asleep had part of its handshake time taken away, and below a one-second timeout was refused.** The daemon reads the clock, then waits in `poll()` for up to a second, and a connection accepted when `poll()` returned had its handshake deadline counted from the reading taken before the wait. At the default `handshake_timeout_ms = 10000` a login lost up to a second of its ten. The configuration accepts values down to 100, and at anything under 1000 the deadline of a login that arrived later into the wait than the timeout had passed before it was accepted: the daemon closed it on its next turn and the client reported a failed WebSocket upgrade. Reproduced against the shipped daemon at `handshake_timeout_ms = 500`: five logins spread across the wait, three refused. Whether a login worked depended only on when it arrived. Found by a reviewer agent reading the network path. | v4 (V4-8a's event loop) | Low | `apps/authd/evloop.c` `evloop_run_once`; `apps/authd/authd_main.c` | **FIXED in V4-144.** The loop takes an optional clock, `evloop_set_clock()`, and asks it once when `poll()` returns; the daemon's clock refreshes the two times it hands the connection layer as well, so everything done in one turn still sees one instant. Tests that register no clock keep the time they pass. `test_authd_evloop` pins the accept time, the deadline and the sweep against a clock that disagrees with the argument; `authd_e2e` runs seven logins spread across the wait against the shipped daemon at `handshake_timeout_ms = 600`. Campaign v103, CK1–CK5. **Not pinned:** nothing fails if the daemon's clock refreshes the millisecond time and leaves the wall-clock second stale. | V4-144 |
| F225 | **`authd_client` told the user to have an administrator re-enrol the device when it had not been able to ask the daemon anything.** After an interrupted key rotation the client holds two key files, `.ek` and `.ek.next`, and asks the daemon which one is live by trying a login with each. Any failure of either try was counted as the daemon refusing that key: a mistyped passphrase (neither file decrypts, the daemon is never contacted), a daemon that is down or unreachable, or a damaged `.ek.next`. In each case the client printed "neither … authenticates. NOTHING has been deleted; an administrator must re-enrol this device". Nothing was deleted, so no key was lost, but the instruction was wrong and following it throws away a working enrolment. Reproduced against the shipped client with a wrong passphrase and with no daemon listening. Found by reading `client_resolve_key`. | v4 (the rotation recovery of V4-9) | Low | `apps/authd/client_cli.c` `client_resolve_key`, `client_open_session_ex` | **FIXED in V4-146.** The client records whether a try got as far as handing the daemon its key. Only a failure after that point is the daemon's answer. The refusal now names each file's status and says one of four things: neither file could be opened; the daemon could not be asked (with the reason) and to run it again; one key is refused and the other file will not open; or, as before, both keys were refused and the device must be re-enrolled. `authd_e2e` runs all four against the shipped binaries. Campaign v104, RK1–RK6. **Not changed:** a lockout or rate-limit answer from the daemon is indistinguishable from a refused key by design (the responder answers uniformly), so it is still reported as refused. The browser client's `resolveHeld` has the same shape and is not yet examined. | V4-146 |
| F226 | **The browser client reported "neither the current nor the pending key authenticates" when it had not been able to ask the daemon.** The same defect as F225, in `web/client/flows.mjs`. With a pending key in the store (an interrupted rotation), the login and rotation flows try each key against the daemon, and `probe()` turned every failure into "not accepted": a mistyped passphrase, a site that was not reachable, or a pending record that would not open all ended in `RecoveryRefused`. Nothing in the store was changed, but the page told a person whose key was fine that neither of their keys worked. Reproduced in `tests/web_flows.mjs` against the real daemon before the fix (five checks failed). Found by reading, after F225. | v4 (V4-13c's flows) | Low | `web/client/flows.mjs` `probe`, `resolveHeld` | **FIXED in V4-147.** A probe now says `live`, `refused` (both handshake frames went and the core read the daemon's answer), `unopened` (the envelope did not open, nothing sent) or `unasked` (anything else). `RecoveryRefused` is thrown only for two refusals. Everything else throws the new `RecoveryUnresolved`, whose message says which case it was, carries the underlying reason and keeps "nothing has been changed". `tests/web_flows.mjs` pins the three cases, the unchanged store and that the same identity then logs in; five hand mutations of the fix each fail a named check (JavaScript is outside the campaign runner). **Not pinned:** a daemon that closes after the first frame is classed `unasked`, and nothing fails if that boundary moves from two frames to one. **Not changed:** a daemon that goes away between receiving the key and answering still reads as a refusal, as in the CLI. | V4-147 |
| F227 | **A rotation completed by the recovery left the device's `.pub` naming the old key.** `authd_client rotate` publishes the new `.pub` only after the daemon's acknowledgement, and deletes its temporary copy on every other exit. When the acknowledgement is lost, the next login or rotate finds `.ek.next`, learns from the daemon that it is the live key and renames it over `.ek`; the `.pub` was not touched and went on naming the superseded key, with no message. The `.pub` is what an administrator enrols from: the next enrolment of that device from its own file installs a key the device no longer holds, and its logins then fail with nothing to say why. Reproduced in `authd_e2e` by the state an interrupted rotation leaves (the old `.pub` beside a committed `.ek.next`); the lost-acknowledgement path that produces that state was read, not driven. Found by reading `cmd_client_rotate`. | v4 (V4-9's rotation recovery) | Low | `apps/authd/client_cli.c` `client_resolve_key`, `cmd_client_rotate` | **FIXED in V4-149.** The promotion republishes the `.pub` from the promoted key (`client_republish_pub`: written beside the key, renamed into place, as rotate does). A failure to write it is reported, "it still names the OLD key", and does not fail the login: the key is already promoted. Rotate and the recovery share one function for the `.pub` path. `authd_e2e`'s PROMOTE leg starts from the stale `.pub` and compares the file's key with the fingerprint the daemon reports. Campaign v105, PB1–PB4. **Not pinned:** the check that no temporary `.pub` is left behind has no mutation; the failure message is not exercised. | V4-149 |
| F228 | **`EXCHANGE` issued a token on a guessed role when the user's record could not be read.** After a login code is consumed, `EXCHANGE` reads the user's role, which sets the token's absolute and idle lifetimes (operator 8 h and 30 min, user 12 h and 1 h). The status of that read was discarded and the role left at its default, `user`. A store read that failed, or a role the store does not recognise, therefore produced a token marked `role=user` with a user's lifetimes, for an operator as for anyone else, where spec §8 (erratum 96) says a request whose store operation failed is answered `internal` and nothing else. The site sees `role=user`, so no privilege is gained; what is lost is the operator's shorter lifetimes and the rule that a failure is never answered as a success. Reproduced in `test_authd_localapi` with a role value the store does not know, written from a second connection. Found by reading `h_exchange` for discarded statuses, after F222 and F223. | v4 (V4-9's local API) | Low | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_exchange` | **FIXED in V4-150.** A role lookup that does not return OK answers `ERR code=internal` and no token is issued. The login code is already spent by then, so the user logs in again; the test pins that too. Campaign v106, EX1–EX2. **Not pinned:** a read that fails inside SQLite (I/O, a lock) is not driven, only an unreadable role value; both leave by the same status check. The two other discarded store statuses in the daemon were read and left: `store_touch_last_seen` after a handshake is a best-effort timestamp, and `store_audit_tail` in `audit-verify` only supplies the entry count it prints. | V4-150 |
| F229 | **The daemon started on a store that another server key wrote, and appended to its audit chain.** The audit chain is keyed from the store root key sealed in `server.ek` (spec §9.2); opening a store checks nothing against that key. A store backup restored beside a newly made `server.ek`, or a `key_path` naming another server's key, therefore started normally and logged `event=started`. Every client was then refused at `server-hello: signature`, since they pin the other key, with nothing in the daemon's output to say why; and each entry the daemon wrote went into the chain under the wrong key, after which `audit-verify` fails under either key. `authd_admin init` has refused to re-key an existing store for this reason since V4-9; the daemon had no such check. Measured with the shipped binaries: a backup beside a new key started, the enrolled device's login failed, and `audit-verify` answered `audit-chain-corrupt`; the same backup beside its own key started and the device logged in. Found while checking what the runbook's backup step is enough to restore. | v4 (V4-9's store and daemon) | Medium | `apps/authd/authd_main.c` start-up; `apps/authd/store/store.c` | **FIXED in V4-151.** At every start the daemon recomputes the last audit entry's MAC under its own key (`store_audit_tail_check`: one row, one MAC). On a mismatch it prints the store's path, `audit-chain-corrupt` and what that means, exits 1, and binds nothing (spec §9.2, erratum 97). An empty chain passes. `test_authd_store` pins the check (the right key, another key, an edit at the end, an edit elsewhere); `authd_e2e` pins the refusal against the shipped daemon and, as the canary, that a backup beside its own key starts and logs the device in. Campaign v107, TC1–TC4. The runbook's start-failure list names the message. **The limit:** it is not `audit-verify`. An entry altered anywhere but the end, and a chain cut short, still start; the hourly timer is what catches those. **A changed behaviour:** a store whose last entry was altered no longer starts either. That is an outage where there was a running daemon on a tampered store; the owner may prefer a warning. | V4-151 |
| F230 | **`audit-verify` said where a chain failed by printing its length, and its head and count were three separate reads.** The command verified the chain, then read the head MAC and the last entry's sequence number in statements of their own. (a) On a failure it printed `FAILED after N entries` with N the chain's length, wherever the walk had stopped: an edit to the first entry of a long chain, or the wrong `--key`, read as every entry but the last being good. (b) The status of the read that supplied N was discarded: had it failed after a successful verify, the command would have printed `the audit chain verifies (0 entries)` and the note that the store had recorded nothing, with exit 0. (c) Run beside the daemon, as the hourly timer does, an entry appended between the reads gave a `head=` that belonged to an earlier entry than the count beside it, and that pair is what the runbook says to forward off the host. (a) reproduced in `authd_e2e` before the fix; (b) and (c) are read from the code, not driven. Found by a reviewer agent reading `cmd_audit_verify`. | v4 (V4-10c's command) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_cli.c` `cmd_audit_verify`; `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_audit_verify` | **FIXED in V4-154.** `store_audit_verify_report` returns, from the one statement that verifies, how many entries verified and the MAC of the last of them. The command prints that head and count on success, and on a chain failure `FAILED at entry N: the N−1 before it verify` beside the stored head; a store error is reported as the store's, not the chain's. `test_authd_store` pins the report on a whole chain, an edited first entry and an edited last entry; `authd_e2e` pins the message for both. Campaign v108, VR1–VR4. **Not pinned:** that the head printed on success is the walk's and not a second read (the two are equal on a store nobody is writing). | V4-154 |
| F231 | **`authd_admin pseudonym` printed a pseudonym under a server key the store was not written with.** The journal's `idh=` is keyed from the store root key in `server.ek` and the store's id. Given another server's key, or a newly made one, with a passphrase that opens it, the command printed a well-formed `idh=` and exited 0. That value is on no line of any journal, so an operator looking for a user's activity finds nothing and reads it as no activity. F193 closed the same wrong answer for a mistyped `--store`; a wrong `--key` was open, and F229's check ran only in the daemon. Reproduced with the shipped tool and a second `init`'s key: `idh=60f85576534bea71`, exit 0. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-13d's command) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_cli.c` `cmd_pseudonym` | **FIXED in V4-155.** The command makes F229's check before it derives anything: when the store's last audit entry was not written under the key given, it prints nothing on stdout, says so by name (`audit-chain-corrupt`, and that such a pseudonym is in no journal) and exits 1. `authd_e2e` pins it with the second server key. Campaign v109, PS1–PS2. **The limit:** a store with no audit entries cannot say which key it belongs to, and the command answers as before; a daemon that has only ever seen unknown handles has logged pseudonyms from such a store. | V4-155 |
| F232 | **`authd_admin keygen-server` left a sealed key with no public half when `--pub` could not be written.** The command sealed the key at `--key` first and wrote `--pub` second, and the public-file writer refuses an existing path. A retry was then refused on that key (`already-exists`); and the old `.pub` beside a key it does not match, handed to clients, fails every login at `server-hello: signature`. `init` has looked first since F195; `keygen-server` did not. Reproduced in `test_authd_cli` before the fix. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-9's command) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_cli.c` `cmd_keygen_server` | **FIXED in V4-157.** Both paths are checked before a key is generated: an existing `--key` or `--pub` is refused by name and nothing is written. If the public half still cannot be written afterwards (a directory that cannot be written to), the command says the key was written and its public half was not, and to remove it. `test_authd_cli` pins the refusal and that no key was sealed. Campaign v110, KS1–KS2. **Not pinned:** the second message; nothing in the suite makes the public write fail after the check passes. | V4-157 |
| F233 | **An `init` that failed after writing told the operator to remove some of what it left.** `init` is not resumable and refuses to run over a passphrase file, key, public key or store. F195's row said a later failure names all three files; that held for one branch. Reproduced in `test_authd_cli` by a directory where SQLite's write-ahead log must go. Campaign v110, IL1–IL2. **Not pinned:** the three other call sites, which the suite has no way to make fail. | V4-157 |
| F234 | **A reference site given `--authd-ws` without a port started, and ended at the first WebSocket upgrade.** The site split `--authd-ws` at each upgrade, not at start. With `127.0.0.1` and no `:port`, `net.connect(NaN, …)` throws `ERR_SOCKET_BAD_PORT` synchronously inside the `'upgrade'` listener, outside every catch: the process ended, for any client that asked to upgrade `/authd/v1`. The same class as F136 and F213, reached through an operator's typing mistake. A bracketed IPv6 address split at its first colon into host `[` and port 0. Only used without a proxy; behind Caddy `/authd/v1` never reaches the site. The throw was shown with the one expression in isolation; the checks failed against the unfixed site because it started. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-13c's site) | Low | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` the `'upgrade'` handler, `startSite` | **FIXED in V4-158.** `hostPort()` parses the address once, in `startSite`: `host:port` or `[v6]:port`, the port 1–65535 in digits. | F235 | **A "Rotate key" that failed before it reached the daemon left a pending key in the browser.** The browser flow seals the new key and stores it as pending before the login that carries `ROTATE`, and that login is the first time the typed passphrase meets the current key. A mistyped passphrase that the policy accepts, or a daemon that could not be reached, failed the login and left the new key staged. No key was lost. But every later login found a pending key, ran the interrupted-rotation probe (a second handshake and a second Argon2id) and told the person a rotation had been interrupted, until some rotation succeeded. Reproduced in `tests/web_flows.mjs` before the fix (six checks). Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-13c's flow) | Low | `web/client/flows.mjs` `rotateHeld` | **FIXED in V4-159.** If that login fails there is no session, so `ROTATE` cannot have left and the daemon cannot hold the new key; the staged key is discarded on that proof and the error is rethrown. Once a session exists the old rule stands: an unacknowledged rotation keeps its key. `tests/web_flows.mjs` pins both failures (nothing pending, the store byte-identical, the next login ordinary). Three hand mutations; the third (also discarding after a session) survived at first, because nothing drove a lost acknowledgement through `flows.rotate`. A test now does: the daemon commits, the ACK is lost, the key is kept and the next login completes the rotation. **Not changed:** the CLI opens the current key before it seals `.ek.next`, and does not have this. | V4-159 |
| F236 | **A configuration refused for what it lacked did not say what that was, and two sockets on one path passed the check.** (a) Eight different conditions returned `missing-required-key` with no line and no name: each of the four required keys, no listener, `proxy_protocol` or `proxy_uids` without `listen_unix`, `site_socket` without `site_uids`, and `proxy_protocol = v2` without `proxy_uids`. Both binaries printed the file's path and those two words. The runbook's table said the message names the setting and line, and the last two are the settings its step 7 tells the operator to edit. (b) `listen_unix`, `site_socket` and `admin_socket` were never compared. Two of them on one path passed `--check-config`; at start the first bound and the second found a listener there, the daemon's own, and failed as `in-use` with exit 1, which the runbook explains as another daemon running. Under `Restart=on-failure` that repeats. (a) reproduced with both shipped binaries; (b) read, and now refused before it can be driven. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-8a's parser; F92's rules in V4-14c) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_config.c`; `apps/authd/authd_main.c`; `apps/authd/authd_cli.c` | **FIXED in V4-160.** A whole-file refusal carries what it is about (`authd_config_error_detail`), and both binaries print it: `missing-required-key: site_uids (site_socket is set)`. | F237 | **A double click on "Log in" failed a correct login, and a page action that threw could leave the page silent.** The pages attached plain async listeners with no guard, so a second click started a second run beside the first. The same race failed "New recovery codes", and on "Rotate key" the second run reported `passphrase-policy` after a rotation that had succeeded. Separately: "Log out" had no catch, so a logout that failed showed nothing, which on a shared machine reads as logged out; the passphrase stayed in its field when an action failed before reaching the step that emptied it (F144's rule); and the account page said "log in first" when the cause was a 503 it had just described. The double click was reproduced in headless Chromium before the fix: both runs failed on a correct passphrase. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-13c's pages) | Low | `web/pages/common.mjs`, `login.mjs`, `account.mjs`, `enroll.mjs`, `recover.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-162.** `action(id, run)` in `common.mjs` gives every button one run at a time: the button is disabled while a run is in progress, further clicks do nothing, and whatever the run throws is reported. Each action reads and empties its secret field before its first await. The account page says the service is not answering when that is why there is no session. `tests/browser_e2e.mjs` pins the double click (one result, a login, the button usable again), the emptied field on an early failure, and a failed logout being reported with the session still there. Four hand mutations each fail a named check. **Not fixed:** a page whose start-up fails (IndexedDB blocked, the module not loading) still shows nothing and has dead buttons. **Not pinned:** the 503 wording, and the guard on the buttons other than "Log in" (they share the one function). | V4-162 |
| F238 | **Two statements about the deployment said what the code does not.** (a) The unit's comment and spec §16 gave 4136 as the most open files a configuration can require. That is the figure at the default `max_local_slots = 8`; the setting's bound is 64, so the most is 4096 + 64 + 32 = 4192. (b) `deploy/authd.conf.example` said systemd creates `/run/mldsa-authd/` "owned by this service with the proxy's group". The unit has `Group=mldsa-authd` and the directory takes that group; the proxy is made a member of it (runbook step 4). Neither changes what an operator must do: no `LimitNOFILE` is needed either way, and the runbook's steps were right. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-123's limit text; V4-11's example) | Low | `deploy/mldsa-authd.service`; `deploy/authd.conf.example`; spec §16 | **FIXED in V4-161**, documents only: 4192 in the unit and the spec (erratum 98), and the example says whose group it is and how the proxy gets in. Checked against `AUTHD_SLOTS_MAX`, `AUTHD_LOCAL_SLOTS_MAX`, `FDLIMIT_OVERHEAD` and the unit's `Group=`. | V4-161 |
| F239 | **A store read that failed during a handshake was answered as "not enrolled" and logged as nothing, or as a cause that had not happened.** At `ClientHello` the daemon looks the handle up, and every answer but "found" pinned the decoy: a database error or a damaged row as much as an unknown handle. On the wire that is what §7.3 wants. But nothing was logged: an enrolled device was refused as `client-auth-failed`, exactly like a wrong key, and a store that could not be read left no line in the journal for an operator to find. The second lookup, at `ClientAuth` (F158), logged any failure as `client-auth-key-changed`. The F222/F228 shape, a discarded status, on the protocol path. Reproduced in `test_authd_localapi` with a key column of the wrong length: the login fails and the captured journal has no store error. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-8's flow; V4-79's re-check) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` `authd_conn_resolve_pin`, `on_client_hello`, the F158 re-check | **FIXED in V4-163.** The lookup's status is kept. A status that is neither "found" nor "not found" is logged `store-read-failed` at ERROR with the store's status in `detail=`; the handshake still proceeds with the decoy, so the wire says nothing new. At `ClientAuth` a failed read closes the connection under the same event and is no longer called a changed key. Spec §7.3 and §15, erratum 99 (v1.57). `test_authd_localapi` pins both sites: at ClientAuth (the store damaged after the handshake's first lookup and before the daemon reads the ClientAuth) the event is logged and `client-auth-key-changed` is not; at ClientHello the event at ERROR with `store-corrupt`, a ServerHello still sent, the handshake ending at ClientAuth, no login code. Campaign v112, SR1–SR4. | V4-163 |
| F240 | **A page that could not start showed nothing and had dead buttons.** The first statement of the login, enroll and account pages is `await boot()`, which loads the WebAssembly module and opens IndexedDB.
