The account page's `fetch('/api/me')` was uncaught in the same place. Reproduced in headless Chromium with IndexedDB made to throw before the page's script ran: all three pages reported nothing. Noted as not fixed in F237's row. | v4 (V4-13c's pages) | Low | `web/pages/common.mjs` `boot`; `web/pages/account.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-164.** `boot()` reports the failure where the page reports everything else, with the reason and what usually causes it, and then throws so the page still stops. The account page treats a site it could not reach as that, not as "Not logged in". `tests/browser_e2e.mjs` pins the three pages with storage blocked, and that the login page works once it is not. One hand mutation (the report removed) fails all three checks. **Not pinned:** the module failing to load, and the account page's unreachable-site wording. | V4-164 |
| F241 | **`VERIFY` answered `role=operator` for a user whose role could not be read.** `store_verify_token` zeroes its result, then converts the role with the conversion's status discarded. A role value the store does not know left the result untouched, and the zero value of the role type is `OPERATOR`. An ordinary user's valid token was therefore verified as `OK … role=operator`, to a site that grants by that field. F228 was the same discarded status in `EXCHANGE`, where the default was `user`; here the guess is the more privileged role, and F228's row did not list this site among those it read. The two id columns were copied with their statuses discarded as well. Reaching it needs a role value the store's own writes never produce (a damaged or hand-edited row); nothing on the network can cause it. Reproduced in `test_authd_localapi` with F228's own fixture: `VERIFY` answered `role=operator`. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-9b's tokens) | Medium | `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_verify_token`; `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_verify` | **FIXED in V4-166.** A token that would verify, on a row whose role or ids could not be read, returns `store-corrupt` and `VERIFY` answers `internal` (spec §8, erratum 96); the result's role is never left at the privileged zero. The refusals come first, so a revoked device or a disabled user is still told as that. `test_authd_localapi` pins it with the role unreadable (not `role=operator`, `internal`) and readable again (`role=user`). Campaign v113, VT1–VT2. **Read and left:** `store_list_users` lists an unreadable role as `user`, the unprivileged guess, in an administrator's listing. **Not pinned:** the two id columns. | V4-166 |
| F242 | **The journal did not mark a device added to an existing account on the site's word.** Spec §10.3 says a site-mediated re-enrollment, `ENROLL via=site` for a person who has lost their device and has no recovery code, "is logged at WARN as `enroll-without-continuity`, because it is the one path with no cryptographic continuity at all". The daemon never wrote that event: such an enrollment was logged `enroll` at INFO, the same line as a first signup. The name was also missing from §15's event list, so the vocabulary gate, which compares that list with the daemon, could not see it. An operator reading the journal for accounts that gained a device without any of the account's keys or codes being used had nothing to look for. Reproduced in `test_authd_localapi`: a second device added to an existing user logged no such line. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (spec V4-3; the handler of V4-9) | Low | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_enroll`; spec §10.3, §15 | **FIXED in V4-167.** A `via=site` enrollment that adds a device to a user who already exists logs `enroll-without-continuity` at WARN after `enroll` and `enroll-fp`. A new user's first device and an idempotent repeat do not. The event is in §15 (erratum 100, v1.58), so the gate now holds it both ways. `test_authd_localapi` pins the three cases. Campaign v114, EC1–EC2. **Note:** `authd_admin enroll-operator` for an existing operator is such an enrollment and is logged the same way. | V4-167 |
| F243 | **A recovery enrollment that named the device the user already had was reported as a recovery.** `ENROLL via=recovery ticket=` with the user's own existing handle and key enrolls nothing: the store rolls its transaction back, so the ticket is not spent, and returns OK. The handler could not tell that from an enrollment. It answered a plain `OK fp=`, where spec §8 says a byte-identical re-enrollment by the handle's own user is `OK fp= idempotent=1`, and it logged `enroll-recovery` and `enroll-recovery-fp` at WARN, with no audit entry behind them, because nothing had happened. A site told `OK` believes the ticket spent; the ticket was good for the rest of its ten minutes. F48 fixed the neighbouring case (another user's handle), and the owner's own was tested only for `via=site`. Reproduced in `test_authd_localapi`. Found by a reviewer agent. | v4 (V4-9d's ticket enrollment) | Low | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_enroll`; `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_enroll_via_ticket` | **FIXED in V4-169.** `store_enroll_via_ticket_ex` reports that nothing was enrolled; the handler then answers `OK fp= idempotent=1` and logs `enroll-idempotent` and `enroll-fp` at INFO, as the `via=site` path does. The ticket is still not spent on it. `test_authd_localapi` pins the reply, the log, and that the same ticket then enrolls a new device. Campaign v115, RI1–RI4. **Not changed:** the reference site clears its copy of the ticket on any `OK`; its recovery page always enrolls a new key, so it does not reach this. | V4-169 |
| F244 | **No token carried the handshake id the spec says it is bound to.** Spec §11: a token is "bound to `{user_id, handle, handshake_id, issued_at, expires_at, idle_expires_at}`". The tokens table has the column, `tokens_issue` takes the value, and `EXCHANGE`, the only caller, passed NULL: the login code's own handshake id was never read back when the code was consumed. Every token's `handshake_id` was NULL. Nothing reads the column, so no check was weakened; what was missing is the record that ties a session to the handshake that opened it, which is what an investigation of a stolen token starts from. Shown by reading the store after an `EXCHANGE`. Found by a reviewer agent; noted in V4-168 as a spec-against-code question. | v4 (V4-9b's tokens; spec V4-3) | Low | `apps/authd/localapi.c` `h_exchange`; `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_consume_login_code_ex` | **FIXED in V4-170**, on the code's side: the spec's sentence was right. `store_consume_login_code_hs` returns the consumed code's handshake id and `EXCHANGE` passes it to `tokens_issue`. `test_authd_localapi` reads the store from a second connection: the token row has a 16-byte handshake id and it equals the login code's. Campaign v116, HS1–HS3. **Not changed:** nothing verifies or reports the column yet; `VERIFY`'s reply does not carry it. | V4-170 |
| F245 | **CI's two fuzz gates could pass without a single input having been fuzzed.** Both steps were `run_fuzz.sh … \| tee LOG` followed by a grep for `crashes=[1-9]\|artifacts=[1-9]`. With no `pipefail` the pipe's status was `tee`'s, so the runner's own exit code was discarded; and the grep asked only whether a crash had been printed, never whether anything had run. Three ways to a green step with nothing fuzzed: a target with no `FUZZ_MAXLEN` (the runner refuses to start, exit 2, no summary lines); a fuzzer that dies without one of the grepped words, an out-of-memory kill among them (the summary reads `runs=?`, exit 1); and the runner's `SKIP … not built`, exit 0. The nightly's step carried a comment saying the runner never exits nonzero on a crash; it does. The nightly's fuzz matrix is written by hand and nothing compared it with `FUZZ_TARGETS`, so a new target would never have had its 600 seconds. Shown: the old grep on a log from a run that refused to start is green. Found by a reviewer agent reading the verification apparatus for gates that cannot fail. | v4 (V4-14b's fuzz jobs) | Medium | `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `nightly.yml`; `tests/fuzz/run_fuzz.sh` | **FIXED in V4-172.** Both steps run under `set -o pipefail` and end with `tests/fuzz/check_fuzz_log.sh`, which requires for every expected target exactly one summary line with a run count above zero and `crashes=0 artifacts=0`, and no `FAIL:` or `SKIP:` line. A new CTest, `fuzz_log_gate`, feeds that gate a clean log and ten that must be refused, checks the nightly matrix against `FUZZ_TARGETS` both ways, and checks that both workflows still run the fuzzers under `pipefail` and end with the gate. Five hand mutations (of the gate, of a workflow, of the matrix): four fail a named check, and the fifth showed two checks covering one case, which now has fixtures of its own. A real 60-second smoke of all twelve targets passes the gate. | V4-172 |
| F246 | **Opening an in-memory store created a file called `:memory:`.** Since F200, `store_open` creates the store's file itself, mode 0600, before SQLite opens it. It did that for SQLite's in-memory name too: every `store_open(":memory:", …)` left an empty file named `:memory:` in the working directory. The fuzz targets open one each, so every build tree had one under `tests/fuzz/`, and a fuzz run from the repository root left one there. Harmless in itself; it is a file the program did not mean to make. Found as an untracked file after the smoke run that verified F245. | v4 (V4-111's F200 fix) | Low | `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_open` | **FIXED in V4-172.** The pre-creation is skipped for `:memory:`. `test_authd_store` opens one and checks no such file appears. Campaign v117, MM1; v90's SC3, which anchored on the changed line, is re-anchored and still killed by its named check. | V4-172 |
| F247 | **The gate that checks the specification's numbers against the tree passed when it had derived nothing, and looked for four values as bare numbers.** `tools/audit/check_spec_constants.sh` reads each size from a `#define` and looks for it in the document. Its reader matches one spelling, `NAME <digits>`. A define rewritten as an expression derived the empty string, and `grep -F ""` matches every file: the row printed `ok` with nothing checked. The §17 rows degraded the same way to patterns such as `" h"` and `"default "`. Separately, the handshake id's length, the handle's length, the identity limit and the envelope header's size were searched for as the bare numbers 16, 34, 64 and 67 anywhere in the specification, where each occurs many times: the handshake id could become 32 bytes in the tree and the gate still found "32". The script's own comment names this defect for §10.3 and scopes those checks; these four were not. Shown on copies of the tree before the fix: six changes, the gate passed every one. Found by a reviewer agent reading the verification apparatus. | v4 (V4-3's gate) | Medium | `tools/audit/check_spec_constants.sh` | **FIXED in V4-173.** Every derived value passes through `req`, which fails by name on anything that is not a number, and `check` refuses an empty pattern. The four bare numbers are looked for in the phrase that states each (`(34 bytes)`, `` `WIRE_ID_MAX_LEN` (64) ``, `Bytes [0, 67) are the AEAD's associated data`, `handshake_id (16)`). A new CTest, `spec_constants_gate`, runs the gate on the tree (must pass) and on six doctored copies (each must fail); an edit that changes nothing fails the test, so a fixture cannot go stale silently. Three hand mutations of the gate each turn that test red. **Not changed:** the reader still knows one spelling of a define; a new spelling is now a named failure, not a silent pass. | V4-173 |
| F248 | **The reference site decoded a request body one chunk at a time, so a character whose bytes arrived in two chunks was read as two replacement characters.** `body()` in `examples/site-node/server.mjs` appended each `data` chunk to a string. A two-byte character split by the network became U+FFFD twice. The invite is compared by digest, so a correct invite holding a non-ASCII character was refused whenever a chunk boundary fell inside it; the same holds for any non-ASCII field. Bytes that were not UTF-8 at all were replaced and then compared, not refused. Reproduced before the fix: one `POST /api/signup` with the invite `pässwörd-é`, sent whole, 200; the same bytes written in two pieces 150 ms apart with the cut inside `ä`, 403. Found by a reviewer agent (second run). | v4 (V4-13b's site) | Low | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-175.** The chunks are kept as bytes and decoded once at the end, strictly: bytes that are not UTF-8 are 400. `tests/site_sessions.mjs`, six `body:` checks: the control that the cut is inside a character, the canary, the split inside a character, a split between characters, a wrong invite split the same way (403), a lone `0xC3` (400). Hand mutations M1 (decode each chunk) and M2 (not strict) fail the named checks. | V4-175 |
| F249 | **The reference handler queued a request on a connection that had closed, and its promise never settled.** `Authd.request()` in `examples/site-node/authd.mjs` pushed the request and wrote it whatever the socket's state. After `close()`, after `destroy()`, or after the daemon went away, nothing read the line and nothing failed the promise: the socket's `close` had already emptied the queue once. A site that keeps one connection to the daemon, as this file's own tests do, waited for ever on every request after a daemon restart, with no error to act on. Before `connect()` the same call threw a `TypeError` from inside the promise. The reference site opens a connection per request and was not affected; the handler is published to be copied. Reproduced before the fix against a stand-in socket server: `after close(): NEVER SETTLED (2 s)`, the same after `destroy()`. Found by a reviewer agent (third run). | v4 (V4-9a's handler) | Low | `examples/site-node/authd.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-175.** The handler knows whether its connection is open: a request on one that is closed or was never opened is refused `connection-closed`, the error the site already reads as the daemon being away. `close()` fails what was still waiting its turn and keeps the request already written. The socket's `error` is listened to for its whole life. `examples/site-node/authd.test.mjs` (CTest `site_node_handler`), two tests, every wait raced against two seconds; hand mutations M3-M5 and M7 fail them, and M6 showed a line that changed nothing, which was removed. | V4-175 |
| F250 | **An entry put in the pending-handshake ledger while the clock could not be read never expired.** `handshake_pending_insert` computes `deadline = now + ttl`, saturating. The default clock reports `UINT64_MAX` when `clock_gettime` fails, so that "every entry reads as expired" (`handshake.h`). That holds for entries made before the failure. One made during it got the deadline `UINT64_MAX`, and when the clock read again no time could reach that: the entry stayed until a ClientAuth consumed it or three bad signatures closed it, holding a slot of a ledger that never evicts a live entry. Shown before the fix with the test's clock: insert at `UINT64_MAX`, set the clock back to its base, read the entry a thousand lifetimes later: `PENDING_OK`. Not reachable in the daemon as shipped: its clock is the loop's reading, which is 0 on a failed read, never `UINT64_MAX`; a library user with the default clock had it. Found by a reviewer agent (fourth run). | v4 (step 4's ledger) | Low | `src/protocol/handshake.c` | **FIXED in V4-176.** An insert at a clock of `UINT64_MAX` is refused `PENDING_ERR_EXPIRED` and stores nothing; the responder answers it as an internal error. `test_handshake`, four `step4 store:` checks. Campaign v118, UC1-UC2. | V4-176 |

Measured before the fix by re-fencing one block at a time: the gate stayed green for 20 of the runbook's 35 blocks, with each of six spellings. No block in the committed runbook was fenced that way; the next edit could have been. Found by a reviewer agent reading the verification apparatus (fourth run). | v4 (V4-99's rehearsal) | Low | `tools/rehearse_runbook.py` | **FIXED in V4-177.** Every fenced block inside a numbered step must open with exactly ` ```bash ` or be named in `NOT_COMMANDS` with a line it holds and a reason (one block: step 15's two configuration lines). A name that matches nothing, and a block never closed, are refused too. Both the check mode and the full rehearsal refuse. New CTest `runbook_gate`: each of the 35 blocks re-fenced six ways, a second plain block, the named block changed, an unclosed block; ten of its checks fail against the tool as it was. | V4-177 |
| F252 | **A uid allowlist ending in a comma was accepted.** `parse_uids` in `apps/authd/authd_config.c` refuses an empty element, which caught a leading comma (`,33`) and two together (`33,,1001`), but it stepped over a comma and then left the loop if the value ended there. `site_uids = 33,`, a second uid meant and never typed, started the daemon with one uid and no word about it. The result fails closed (one peer fewer is admitted, never one more), so this is a configuration typo accepted, not an access defect. Shown before the fix: `site_uids = 33,` and `site_uids = 33,1001,` both parsed `AUTHD_CFG_OK`. Found by a reviewer agent (first run). | v4 (V4-9's configuration) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_config.c` | **FIXED in V4-178.** A comma that ends the list is `AUTHD_CFG_ERR_VALUE`, reported with its line like any other bad value. `test_authd_evloop`, four `cfg:` checks (the canary of two uids; the trailing comma after one uid and after two; the three forms refused before). Campaign v119, TC1-TC2. | V4-178 |
| F253 | **A temporary file left by an earlier run stopped every later write of a key file, and the sealed key's writer called it the key file existing.** Key files are published by writing `<target>.tmp.<pid>` with `O_EXCL` and linking it into place. A process killed between creating that file and unlinking it leaves it behind. A later process with the same id then failed at the `open`: `publish()` in `apps/authd/keyfile.c` answered `KEYFILE_ERR_EXISTS`, which every caller reports as the key file already being there, with no key file there; `write_new_file()` in `apps/demo_keys.c` (the `.pub`, and the demo tools' key files) answered an I/O error. Nothing removed the leftover, so it lasted until someone found it. Process ids repeat rarely on a host and on every run in a container, where a keygen step is often the same id each time. Shown before the fix: the leftover planted, `keyfile_write_sealed` returned EXISTS and `demo_keys_write_public` an I/O error, with neither target present. Found by a reviewer agent (first run). | v4 (V4-6's key files) | Low | `apps/authd/keyfile.c`, `apps/demo_keys.c` | **FIXED in V4-179.** A file already holding the temporary name is removed and the name created again, still `O_EXCL` and `O_NOFOLLOW`: the name carries this process's id, so no live process but this one can own it. A failure on the temporary file is now an I/O error in both writers; EXISTS is said only of the target. `test_authd_keyfile`, eight `stale temp:` checks. Campaign v120, ST1-ST2. | V4-179 |
| F254 | **The end-to-end test's stop-while-answering leg assumed a 20 ms sleep ends before one Argon2id computation does.** The F219 leg sends a request that costs one recovery computation, sleeps 20 ms, and sends SIGTERM; an attempt whose reply is already there is "void" and repeated, eight times at most. The computation measures about 40 ms on this machine. On a loaded runner the client's sleep overran it every time: main's push CI 43622502645 failed on `macos-26·clang·asan` with `attempts: [void] x 8`, and passed on a rerun of the job. | F261 | **A WebSocket Close did not close the connection.** `ws.c` echoed the Close and moved to its closed stage, where every later byte is read and discarded; nothing told the event loop. The slot stayed ACTIVE, with its address's connection count, until the peer hung up or a deadline fired. RFC 6455 7.1.1 has the server close the TCP connection first, so a client that follows it waits for exactly that. With the shipped `max_conns_per_addr = 8` and `idle_timeout_ms = 60000`, eight such closes from one address (an office behind one NAT) within a minute and the ninth connection is refused 429. Reproduced against a running daemon: upgrade, send a Close, read the echo, and the connection was `STILL OPEN after 6 s`. Found by a reviewer agent (sixth run). | v4 (V4-10a's WebSocket listener) | Medium | `apps/authd/evloop.c`, `apps/authd/conn_io.c` | **FIXED in V4-184.** When a read leaves the WebSocket layer ended, the loop marks the slot draining: what is queued is written (the echo, and any reply) and the slot is closed. The same run now reads the echo and `EOF after 0.00 s`. `test_authd_evloop`, `wsclose:` checks over a real loopback connection with ten-minute deadlines, so only the Close can have released the slot. Campaign v122, WC1. | V4-184 |
| F262 | **A WebSocket control reply was dropped whenever an earlier one was still unsent.** `queue_ctrl` returned at once if the reply buffer held unsent bytes, and that buffer also carries the 101. Two Pings in one read: one Pong, for the first, where RFC 6455 5.5.3 allows skipping only the earlier. A Ping and a Close in one read: the Pong, no Close echo, and (F261) a connection left open, so the client waited for an echo that was never coming. Reproduced against a running daemon: both. Found by a reviewer agent (sixth run). | v4 (V4-10a's WebSocket listener) | Low | `apps/authd/ws.c`, `apps/authd/ws.h` | **FIXED in V4-184.** Control replies are appended behind what is unsent, in a buffer of two maximum control frames plus four bytes. A Pong that does not fit is still dropped; it may never take the last four bytes, which are a Close echo's, and a Close echo is never dropped. `test_authd_ws`, five checks (two Pongs in order; Pong then Close echo; the buffer filled exactly to the kept room; the echo still queued then; a reply behind a half-written one). Campaign v122, WC2-WC4. `fuzz_ws` 600 s on the changed parser. | V4-184 |

It then sent the argument's text. The daemon reads at most four characters of digits; a zero-padded count of five or more is a valid number to the first check and too long for the second. | F271 | **The example proxy configuration and the reference site forwarded the browser's cookies to the daemon, which closes without a word on an upgrade request over 4,096 bytes.** `WS_UPGRADE_MAX` bounds the header block the daemon will read before the WebSocket upgrade; a longer one is a protocol error and the connection is closed with nothing sent. `deploy/Caddyfile.example` proxied `/authd/v1` with every request header, and the site's own relay copied `rawHeaders` whole. A browser sends every cookie it holds for the domain with the upgrade, so a visitor carrying a few kilobytes of them (single sign-on, consent strings, analytics on a parent domain) could neither enroll nor log in, and saw only a failed connection; the daemon logged `closed-protocol`. The daemon uses no cookie, and the site's session cookie was being handed to it for nothing. Measured on a running daemon: a request of 3,960 bytes is answered 101, one of 4,260 bytes is closed. With standard output closed, or on a full disk, or into a pipe whose reader had gone, a caller got status 0 and nothing to exchange. Shown before the fix (campaign v124's mutant is the code as it was): `login ... >&-` exits 0. Found by a reviewer agent (eighth run). | v4 (V4-9b's client) | Low | `apps/authd/client_cli.c` | **FIXED in V4-194.** The write and the flush are checked; on failure the command says the login succeeded and the code is lost, and exits 1. `authd_e2e`, one check. | F273 | **A daemon that refuses its configuration is restarted every two seconds for ever, and the unit never shows as failed.** `deploy/mldsa-authd.service` has `Restart=on-failure`, `RestartSec=2s`, no `RestartPreventExitStatus=` and no start limit. The daemon exits 3 for a configuration, key or passphrase it cannot use, from `ExecStartPre=` (`--check-config`) or from its own start; nothing about a restart can change that outcome. Measured by the rehearsal on Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04 (V4-193): a bad value appended to `authd.conf`, restart, twelve seconds later `active=activating sub=auto-restart result=exit-code restarts=5 listed-failed=0`. A monitor watching for failed units sees nothing; with a wrong passphrase each cycle also spends the 256 MiB Argon2id. Read from the unit by a reviewer agent (eighth run), then measured. | v4 (V4-99's unit) | Medium | `deploy/mldsa-authd.service`, `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` | **OPEN: the owner's decision.** The runbook's step 9 now says what this looks like and where the reason is (V4-195). Changing the unit is a choice between two failures: stopping after one refused start (the pre-start check made informational, `RestartPreventExitStatus=3`) makes the fault visible and stops the loop, but a cause that would have cleared by itself, such as a filesystem not yet mounted at boot, then leaves the daemon down until someone acts; retrying as now heals that case and hides the other. The measurement stays in the rehearsal whichever is chosen. Review 66 (V4-306) found a third way the decision had not weighed: a start limit, `StartLimitIntervalSec=120` and `StartLimitBurst=5` in `[Unit]` with `RequiresMountsFor=/var/lib/mldsa-authd`. A late mount is still retried; a typo ends as `failed` after about five tries; systemd's default (5 starts in 10 s) never trips because each cycle takes over 2 s. Recommended now: that, which leaves only its numbers to the owner | V4-193, V4-195 |
| F274 | **An interrupted run of `deploy/fetch-deps.sh` made every later run fail, and a stalled download never fell through to the mirror.** The script clones liboqs into `liboqs.git.part` and renames it when the clone is done. Interrupted, the directory stays, and `git clone` refuses a destination that is not empty: each rerun stopped at `fatal: destination path ... already exists`, until someone deleted a directory the script never mentions. Separately, `curl` was run with no time limit, so a source that accepts the connection and sends nothing held the script for ever and the second source in its list was never tried. Shown before the fix: a non-empty `liboqs.git.part` planted, the script stops at git's message. Found by a reviewer agent (eighth run). | v4 (V4-11's script) | Low | `deploy/fetch-deps.sh` | **FIXED in V4-196.** The leftover is removed before the clone, and each download has a connect timeout of 20 s and a total of 600 s. Run for real over the planted leftover: liboqs cloned and matched to its pinned commit, both archives downloaded and matched to their pins, exit 0; run again on the finished cache, three `ok` lines and nothing fetched. No automated test: it needs the network, and CI's offline-build job runs the script on every push. | V4-196 |
| F275 | **Revoking a device, or disabling its user, closed every connection that had so far only named the handle, and so told a peer with no key that the handle was enrolled.** `close_matching` in `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` closed any connection whose `handle` or `user_id` matched, at any stage. Both are filled at the ClientHello, before anything is proved: a peer can send a ClientHello naming any handle, receive the ServerHello and hold the connection until the handshake deadline. For an unknown handle nothing more happens. For an enrolled one, the connection was closed the instant an operator or the site revoked the device or disabled its user. Holding a few such connections told a keyless peer which handles exist, the moment each was revoked, and, because a disable closes by user, which handles belong to the same person. Req 6 has known and unknown identities look the same to such a peer. Shown before the fix: a connection waiting for its ClientAuth, `REVOKE-DEVICE` on the admin socket, the slot released at once; the same for `DISABLE-USER`. Found by a reviewer agent's second read of the protocol core (ninth run). | v4 (V4-15a's live-session close) | Medium | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-197.** Only a connection in the serving stage, whose peer has proved it holds the key, is closed. One still waiting for its ClientAuth stays until it sends one or its deadline passes, and its ClientAuth is refused then because the store is asked again (F158). `test_authd_localapi`, `unproven:` checks for a revoke and for a disable: the connection stays, and its ClientAuth afterwards gets no login code. Campaign v125: RS1 (every stage closed again) and RS2 (nothing closed, killed by the existing Req 9 checks). | V4-197 |
| F276 | **The specification, the code's own comment and an audit row described an order of checks for `ROTATE` that the daemon does not use.** §6.3 put `pk_new ≠ pk_old` and "`pk_new` unused" before the two signature verifications. The daemon verifies both signatures and only then calls the store, which makes those checks, and the active-device and pinned-key checks, inside its one transaction. The comment above the code said "reordering would contradict the spec" about a reordering that had happened, and F37 kept open, as an accepted residual, a timing oracle (is this key enrolled somewhere?) that the stated order implies and the real one does not have. The code is the stronger of the two; the documents were wrong, and nothing pinned the order: the one test compared error codes, which are the same either way. Found by a reviewer agent's second read of the protocol core (ninth run). | v4 (V4-9c's rotation; the store's single transaction) | Low | `docs/mldsa-authd-spec.md`, `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-198**, as spec erratum 102 (v1.60): §6.3 states the order the daemon uses and why; the comment says the same; F37 is closed. `test_authd_conn`: a ROTATE naming an in-use key with a `sig_new` that does not verify is refused, the journal says `rotate-sig-new`, and no store refusal is logged. Campaign v126, RO1. No behaviour changed. | V4-198 |
| F277 | **The check cited for "a revoked device cannot rotate" passed with the device-status clause removed.** `store_rotate_key` refuses inside its transaction when the device or its user is not active. Campaign v49c's W7 (both statuses ignored) was killed only by the disabled-user check, which the claim map's D9 does not cite. A revoked device still could not rotate, by the key clause; the device clause was unpinned. Found by a reviewer agent reading the claim map against its tests (tenth run). | v4 (V4-9c's test) | Low | `tests/test_authd_store.c` | **FIXED in V4-200.** A device whose own row says revoked, with its key row left active, must be refused, and accepted again once the row is active. With W7 applied by hand the new check fails. D9 cites it. | V4-200 |
| F278 | **The rotation digest's "both labels" vector was built for one label.** `test_authd_conn` builds the spec 6.3 digest by hand from literal label bytes for `rotate-old` and compares; for `rotate-new` it required only that the digest differ. Any string but rotate-old's passed, and both sides of every round trip share the macro. A wrong label would have interoperated with nothing but this implementation, unnoticed. Shown: the macro changed to `rotate-nxw`, every check passed. Found by a reviewer agent (tenth run). | v4 (V4-9c's test) | Low | `tests/test_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-200.** A second literal vector, for `rotate-new`. Campaign v127, LB1. D8 cites it. | V4-200 |
| F279 | **The end-to-end test looked for a leaked login code in an encoding the daemon never writes.** Its check of the real daemon's journal was one `grep` for the code as the client prints it, base64url. The daemon has no base64 in its logging: a code it leaked would be hex, or raw or escaped bytes. Campaign v49c's W12 (the code logged as a fingerprint) is killed in-process by `test_authd_conn`; applied to the real binary it passed `authd_e2e`. The claim map's D13 cited that line as its one scan of the real process. Found by a reviewer agent (tenth run). | v4 (V4-9's test) | Medium | `tests/authd_e2e.sh` | **FIXED in V4-200.** Both codes the script holds are decoded and looked for in every daemon log: raw, hex in both cases, base64 in both alphabets, whole and as their first and last 16 bytes, after the scan has found a planted copy in each form. With W12 applied by hand: `a login code appears in authd.log`. 23 logs, 11 forms on a clean run. D13 cites it, and the server-passphrase scan of V4-181. | V4-200 |
| F280 | **A binary started with a standard descriptor closed wrote its output into whatever file or socket took that number.** Nothing checked that descriptors 0, 1 and 2 were open. A process started with one closed gets that number back from its next `open()` or `socket()`. On Linux, `authd_client login >&-` connected to the daemon on descriptor 1, completed the login, `printf`ed the single-use login code into that connection, and exited 0: V4-194's check of the write could not see it, because the write succeeded. `authd_admin` would likewise print a listing into the admin socket as if it were commands. A daemon started with standard error closed would send its log lines to whichever file took descriptor 2, and the first file it opens for writing is its store. systemd never starts a unit that way; a hand start or a careless wrapper can. Found by CI, not by reading: run 43648510791, all six Linux jobs, `a login with its standard output closed exited 0` (V4-194's own check, which passed on macOS, where the numbers fall differently). With the fix removed from the daemon and all three closed, its descriptor 0 here was `/dev/urandom`. | v1 (every binary's start) | Medium | `apps/authd/stdfds.c`, `apps/authd_client.c`, `apps/authd/authd_admin.c`, `apps/authd/authd_main.c` | **FIXED in V4-201.** The first thing each `main()` does is open `/dev/null` on any of the three that is closed, so no later descriptor can take its number. The two CLIs then refuse, exit 1, if standard output was the closed one: nothing they print could be read, and for `login` no code is issued. `test_authd_cli`, four `stdfds:` checks in forked children; `authd_e2e`: both CLIs with standard output closed, one with only input and error closed, and a daemon started with all three closed whose descriptors are read back as `/dev/null` three times, which serves, stops with 0 and leaves a store that verifies. V4-194's check now uses a pipe whose reader has gone. Campaign v128, SF1-SF3 (a fourth, the daemon not looking at all, is killed on macOS and is equivalent on Linux, where the campaigns' sanitizer runtime reserves the three descriptors itself: by hand, V4-202); v124 rerun. | V4-201, V4-202 |
| F281 | **The symbolic model's login-`state` property was true because the site's login could never happen in it, and four of its seven properties had no control.** In `formal/authd.pv` the site accepted a login only when presented, on the public channel, with the login code; the device never sent the code anywhere and another query proves the attacker cannot learn it. So the event `siteLoggedIn` was unreachable, and `siteLoggedIn ==> loginStart` held of every run there was, none. Asked directly, ProVerif answered `not event(siteLoggedIn(d,st)) is true`. The claim map cited that query for Req 5's state binding, and three documents said the controls make each proof fail; the daemon authenticating the device, key confirmation, login-code secrecy and this property had none, and the model contained a `CONTROL:state` anchor nothing used. Nothing checked that the honest protocol runs at all. The model and the code do agree on what both handshake signatures cover and on the key derivation's inputs: that was read field by field and is not in question. Found by a reviewer agent comparing the model with the code (eleventh run); reproduced with ProVerif 2.05 in a container. | v4 (V4-4's model) | Medium | `formal/authd.pv`, `formal/gen_controls.py`, `formal/run.sh` | **FIXED in V4-204.** The device posts its code to the site on a channel the attacker cannot read, as the page does under TLS, and the attacker chooses the session it is presented in, which is what login-CSRF is; `state` reaches the daemon on a channel the attacker cannot write. The property is proved of a model in which ProVerif exhibits a login reaching the site (`not event(siteLoggedIn) is false`), and `run.sh` requires that. Four controls added, one per uncontrolled query: all ten now make their query unprovable. `formal/README.md` gained a Limits section: one honest device, no long-term key compromise, records as bare encryption, a completed rotation not exhibited. | V4-204 |
| F282 | **The daemon closed the site's connections whenever more arrived than it had local slots, including for the tenth of a second it spends on one recovery computation.** The site and the operator share `max_local_slots` (8 by default). A local connection that arrived with all of them in use was accepted and closed at once, as a stranger's is on the protocol listener. The daemon is single-threaded: one recovery KDF holds it for 87 to 100 ms (measured), and nothing is released in that time. A site that opens a connection per request, as the reference site does, got a closed connection for every request beyond the pool in that window and answered each 503; beyond 17 in the burst the `connect()` itself failed, the site socket's queue being 16. Measured on Linux in a container, a Release build: with the daemon in one KDF, a burst of 24 non-blocking connects gave 17 connected and 7 `EAGAIN`, a burst of 64 gave 17 and 47, three times each, and the daemon logged 388 `local-accept-refused-no-slot` over the run. An idle daemon took bursts of 24 whole. The question came from a reviewer agent's note on the CLI (eighth run); the mechanism was found by measuring. | v4 (V4-9's local sockets) | Medium | `apps/authd/evloop.c`, `apps/authd/authd_main.c` | **FIXED in V4-205**, with spec erratum 103 (v1.61). A local connection is not accepted while the local pool is full: it waits in the listen queue and is taken when a slot is released, and the loop does not poll that listener meanwhile. The site socket's queue is 128. The event `local-accept-refused-no-slot` is gone; a stranger at a full protocol table is refused as before. `test_authd_localapi`, `localwait:` checks: a late peer at a full pool is neither answered nor closed, the loop sleeps and does not spin, the peer is answered when a slot is released, and of two peers arriving in one turn with one slot free one is answered and the other waits. Campaign v129, LW1-LW2. Measured again on Linux after the fix (decisions, V4-205). | V4-205 |

Where it did look, it matched `crypto.subtle` as a pair: `const { subtle } = crypto`, `globalThis.crypto` and `window.crypto` name no such pair and passed. It also cut each line at the first `//`, including the one in a string such as `'ws://'`, so code after a URL on the same line was not looked at. No page uses any of it today. Shown: a file holding `const { subtle } = crypto; subtle.digest(...)` and, after a `ws://` literal, `window.crypto.getRandomValues(...)`: the gate as it was, `All checks passed`. Found by a reviewer agent (twelfth run). | v4 (V4-13b's gate) | Low | `tests/web_no_crypto.mjs`, `tests/CMakeLists.txt` | **FIXED in V4-207.** Both directories are scanned, and a directory with no JavaScript in it fails. The gate tests itself first: eight uses must be flagged and five harmless lines must not. The same file now gives six failures. | V4-207 |

| F287 | **The bench workflow never ran the bench that measures the decoy flow's timing.** `bench/run_bench.sh` derives its list of suites from the `foreach` in `bench/CMakeLists.txt` and names by hand the executables declared outside it. `bench_authd` was named; `bench_decoy`, declared beside it in V4-65, was not, and `bench.yml` runs only that script. The one measurement of whether an enrolled handle can be told from an unknown one by time (audit F135, the floor of spec 7.3) therefore ran only as an 8-sample smoke in CTest, whose own output says its numbers are meaningless, and when someone ran it by hand. A regression of the floor would have left the bench workflow green. Separately, its count of lookups over the floor relied on a test hook that the daemon skips when it cannot read its clock: the count would then be zero for want of data. Found by a reviewer agent (twelfth run). | v4 (V4-65's bench) | Low | `bench/run_bench.sh`, `bench/bench_decoy.c` | **FIXED in V4-208.** The script runs `bench_decoy` and now refuses to start if any `bench_*` executable declared in `bench/CMakeLists.txt` is missing from its list (shown by taking `decoy` back out: `bench_decoy is declared ... and not run by this script`). The bench requires the hook to have reported each lookup. Run once end to end here: five suites, 83 s; the decoy sections are in the output. The bench reports; it still does not fail on a timing difference, which on shared runners would be noise. | V4-208 |
| F288 | **On server-class shared hardware the pin's 10 µs time floor is below the lookup's 99th percentile.** The floor of spec 7.3 hides the difference between looking up an enrolled handle and an unknown one by making every lookup take at least 10 µs. Its value was set (V4-85, the owner's choice; 50 µs before) as twice the lookup's 99th percentile on the bench machine, about 5 µs. The first run of `bench_decoy` on a GitHub-hosted Xeon 6975P-C VM, Release (bench run 43695441981, 4,000 a class): the unfloored lookup's medians are 7.40 µs enrolled and 6.90 µs unknown (t statistic 271), its pooled 99th percentile 12.0 µs, and 48, 38 and 42 lookups of 4,000 a class ran over the floor, about 1 in 100. Under the floor the medians are equal (10.09 µs, t statistic 1.67). End to end the t statistic is 3.06, below dudect's 4.5, with every cut leaning toward the enrolled handle being slower. So nothing is shown to leak; the premise the value was chosen on does not hold on this kind of host, and the measurement that would settle it (100,000 a class, as was done on the bench machine) has not been made there. Found by running the bench V4-208 added to the workflow. | v4 (V4-85's value) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_conn.h`, `bench/results.md` | **OPEN: the owner's decision.** The numbers are in `bench/results.md` ("The floor on a shared server VM"). The choices: leave 10 µs and accept about 1 unhidden lookup in 100 on such hosts; raise it (50 µs costs every handshake 40 µs more, against about 220 µs end to end there); or make it a configuration setting with the bench as the way to choose it. A larger sample on the runner would say whether the end-to-end lean is real. | V4-210 |
| F289 | **The phone timing tool left its daemon fixture running when its site could not start.** `tools/phone_timing.mjs` starts the fixture (a real daemon with a store and three sockets, told to live for `--minutes` plus half a minute), then the site. With the defaults it served on for fifteen and a half minutes. `tests/browser_e2e.mjs` has the same shape. Shown: the tool started on a port already bound, exit 1, and its fixture still running. Found by a reviewer agent (twelfth run). | v4 (V4-13d's tool) | Low | `tools/phone_timing.mjs`, `tests/browser_e2e.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-211.** The fixture is stopped from the process's `exit` handler, which runs for every way out. `tests/phone_timing_tool.mjs`: the tool with its port taken exits non-zero (the control) and leaves no fixture; the fixture is found by a lifetime argument no other test uses. | V4-211 |
| F290 | **The tools that only read a store changed it, and verifying a backup rewrote the backup.** `store_open_existing`, which `authd_admin audit-verify` and `pseudonym` use and which the header says "creates nothing", opened the file read-write and then did what a creating open does: `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL`, the whole schema as `CREATE ... IF NOT EXISTS`, and a transaction that generated and committed any missing `schema_version` or decoy key. The only earlier gate was that a table named `meta` exists. An operator who records a backup's hash and then verifies its audit chain, which the runbook tells them to do, found a different hash afterwards and `-wal` and `-shm` files beside it. A database that was not a store but had a `meta` table was given eight tables before being refused, and a newer build's store had this build's schema run against it before `SCHEMA` was returned. Shown: a `VACUUM INTO` copy, SHA-256 `2bcbcb38...` before `audit-verify` and `d8c6e22c...` after. Found by a reviewer agent's full read of the store (thirteenth run). | v4 (V4-109's reader) | Medium | `apps/authd/store/store.c`, `apps/authd/store/store.h` | **FIXED in V4-213.** A reader opens read-only, sets no pragma, runs no schema, reads the meta values in a read transaction and refuses a store that lacks one as damaged. The same copy now has the same digest after `audit-verify`, and nothing beside it. `test_authd_store`, `reader:` checks: a backup byte for byte unchanged after open and verify, no side files, a foreign database with a `meta` table refused and unchanged, a store missing `schema_version` or the decoy key refused and not given one. Campaign v130, RD1-RD2; v89 OE2 and OE4 re-anchored, OE3's named check changed, OE5 removed as no longer observable. The end-to-end test's `audit-verify` and `pseudonym` against a live store beside a running daemon pass unchanged. | V4-213 |
| F291 | **The store's schema version was decoded into a signed integer.** Eight bytes from the meta table were shifted into an `int64_t` and refused if greater than this build's version. A value with its top bit set overflows that shift, which is undefined; where it wraps, the result is negative and the store was accepted as an older one. Zero was accepted too. Only this build's version, 1, has ever been written, so no real store is affected. Found by a reviewer agent (thirteenth run). | v4 (V4-9's store) | Low | `apps/authd/store/store.c` | **FIXED in V4-213.** Decoded unsigned; zero and anything above this build's version are `SCHEMA`. `test_authd_store`, three `version:` checks through both opens. Campaign v130, RD3-RD4; v77 SD2 and SD3 re-anchored, SD1 removed as now equivalent. | V4-213 |
| F292 | **The store enrolled a device for a disabled user, or under a role the user does not have, if asked directly.** `store_enroll_device_ex`, the site's enrollment, selected only whether the user existed and left the user's status and role to its caller, which checks them a moment earlier and outside the transaction. `store_rotate_key` and the ticket enrollment re-read the user inside theirs, and their comment says why: the daemon and `authd_admin` are separate processes on one file, and a caller's check is a race. Today nothing writes between the two (the daemon serves one request at a time and the admin tool changes no user), so no request reached it; the rule simply was not the store's. Shown: the function called for a disabled user returned OK and left an active device, live the moment the user is enabled. Found by a reviewer agent's full read of the store (thirteenth run). | v4 (V4-15a's transaction) | Low | `apps/authd/store/store.c` | **FIXED in V4-215.** The user's role and status are read inside the transaction; a user who is not active, or is of another role, is `STORE_ERR_STATE` and nothing is written. The handler's own checks, which choose the error code a site sees, are unchanged and still come first. `test_authd_store`, `enroll-ex:` checks. Campaign v131, EU1-EU2. | V4-215 |
| F293 | **Two runs of the device client on one key file could leave the device without the key the daemon accepts, and a rotation given a very long key path deleted the live key.** `authd_client rotate` looked for `.ek.next` once, spent one key derivation opening `.ek`, and afterwards deleted whatever `.ek.next` was there by then, on a comment saying `.ek` had authenticated when on that path the daemon had not been asked. A second rotation started in between had its newly sealed key deleted; its `ROTATE` then committed and its rename found no file, so `.ek` held a superseded key and the accepted one was on no disk. The browser client got a lock for the same hazard in F218; the CLI had none. Separately, the `.ek.next` name was built with an unchecked `snprintf`: for a `--key` path one byte under `PATH_MAX` it came out equal to the key's own path, the probe found `.ek` live, and the stale-file delete removed the live key (shown: the rotation exited 1 and the key file was gone). Found by a reviewer agent's read of the device client (fourteenth run). | v4 (V4-13a's client) | Medium | `apps/authd/client_cli.c` | **FIXED in V4-217.** A rotation holds an exclusive `flock` on the key's directory from before it reads the key until it exits; a login that finds `.ek.next` holds it while it resolves the file; a run that finds it taken says so and waits. `.ek.next` is deleted only when this run asked the daemon and `.ek` authenticated. A name that does not fit is `path-too-long` before anything is opened. Spec v1.62, erratum 104. `authd_e2e` (a held lock makes a rotation and a resolving login wait; two rotations at once both complete and the device logs in), `test_authd_cli` `long path:`. Campaign v132, KL1-KL4. | V4-217 |

Seen in the clean-suite run that follows a mutant in campaign v132, which reports a flaky suite as `CLEAN-SUITE=FAIL`. | v4 (V4-207's test) | Low | `tests/browser_e2e.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-217.** An attempt given up on before the stand-in ran is repeated with a longer wait (1.5, 5, 15 s); one in which it ran is judged as before. Shown both ways by hand: a first wait of 1 ms is retried and passes; a 1 ms wait alone fails the control by name. | V4-217 |

F272 fixed this for the login code only. Found by a reviewer agent (fourteenth run). | v4 (V4-13a's tools) | Low | `apps/authd/cli_common.h`, `apps/authd/authd_cli.c`, `apps/authd/client_cli.c` | **FIXED in V4-218.** Both tools pass every command's status through `cli_finish_stdout`: a command that returned 0 and whose output cannot be flushed exits 1 and says the command completed but its output could not be written. `authd_e2e`: `list-users` and `keygen` into a pipe whose reader has gone. | F305 | **The browser client copied every inbound WebSocket message into the module, whatever its size.** `feed()` passed the message to `put()`, which allocates and copies, before the core saw its length; `ccw_recv_max` is exported and was never called. The sender is the site's own relay, so this is robustness. Found by the sixteenth review. | v4 (V4-13b's client) | Low | `web/client/authd-client.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-223.** A message longer than the frame buffer (65,565 bytes, more than any frame) is `bad-frame` where it arrives and is never copied; `put()` throws on a refused allocation. `web_flows` `frame:` answers the upgrade from a stand-in and sends 1 MiB: the largest copy is 6,142 bytes. Reverted by hand: red, largest copy 1,048,576. Erratum 107. | V4-223 |
| F306 | **The accept loop had no bound.** `accept_ready` took connections until the queue was empty, and a refusal at a full pool (accept, close, one WARN line) or a rejected peer went round again. Arrivals that outpaced it kept the loop inside `accept_ready`: no slot was read or written, no deadline fired and no `WATCHDOG=1` was sent, so systemd would kill the daemon at 30 s. Reads have been bounded per turn since V4-9 "because fairness across slots matters"; accepts were not. Whether a peer can outpace the daemon was not measured. Campaign v135, AB1. Erratum 108. | V4-224 |
| F307 | **The site listener always won a freed local slot over the admin listener.** Listeners were served in registration order, site before admin, and both draw on `max_local_slots`. With the pool full and the site's queue never empty, each freed slot was refilled by the site before the admin listener was looked at: `authd_admin disable-user`, during an incident on a busy site, waited in its queue without bound. V4-205 recorded the shared pool and an idle site holding it; not this. Campaign v135, LT1 and LT2. Erratum 108. | V4-224 |
| F308 | **A request longer than one read was dropped when its peer hung up.** A hang-up is reported together with the input that preceded it; one turn reads one 4,096-byte chunk, and the turn then closed the slot on `POLLHUP` because no reply was pending. A local request over 4,096 bytes (every `ENROLL` is: the key alone is 3,904 hex characters) from a peer that wrote it and closed was never run; a shorter one was. On macOS the same holds for a peer that only half-closes. Found by the same review. | v4 (V4-56's hang-up rule, F129) | Low | `apps/authd/evloop.c` | **FIXED in V4-224.** The slot is closed on a hang-up only when there was no input to read that turn; the read that finds nothing left returns end-of-file and closes it. `test_authd_evloop` `hangup:` writes 6,000 bytes and closes before the loop turns: the handler runs once, with the whole line. Campaign v135, HU1. Erratum 108. | V4-224 |
| F309 | **The operator's loopback listener is outside the rate limiter, the per-address cap and Req 12.** With `proxy_protocol = v2` and `listen_port` set (the shipped example sets both), the limiter is consulted only when a PROXY preamble settles, which never happens on the raw loopback listener, and no uid check is possible on TCP. Any local process can hold `max_slots` connections open, re-opening them each `handshake_timeout_ms`, so every login through the proxy is refused `accept-refused-no-slot`; or send ClientHellos that each cost one ML-DSA signature with no token charged. §7.3 gives that cost to the rate limiter; the comment in `authd_main.c` says Req 12 refuses connections with no address, which is true of the proxy listener only. F63 covered `proxy_protocol = none`. From reading; not driven. Found by the same review. | v4 (V4-10b's limiter) | Medium | `apps/authd/authd_main.c`, `apps/authd/evloop.c`, `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` | **OPEN: the owner's decision.** Whether an unprivileged local user is an adversary is not in the threat model. The choices: say so in the threat model and leave it (the listener is loopback-only and the example says to set `listen_port = 0` where operators use the browser); put the loopback listener under the limiter as one address, 127.0.0.1, so it is held to `max_conns_per_addr` connections and the per-address rate (operators' failed logins then share one bucket); or give it its own small slot pool so it cannot take the proxy's. Recommended: its own cap on connections, default 8, because that removes the starvation of browser logins without making operators share a rate bucket. | V4-224 |
| F310 | **The per-address rate limit leaked at rates that do not divide the minute.** `refill` added `floor(elapsed * rate / 60)` thousandths of a token and advanced the bucket's clock by `floor(added * 60 / rate)` ms. At `rate_per_min = 100`, one elapsed millisecond added 1 and advanced the clock by 0, so each further call in the same millisecond, refused ones included, added 1 again. Driven in the daemon's own code: an address asking ten times a millisecond for a minute, burst 10, was admitted 669 times at 61 a minute, 729 at 100, 10,629 at 1,000 and 85,208 at 7,777, where the limits are 71, 110, 1,010 and 7,787. The per-address limit then fell back to the global bucket, which one address could spend for everyone. The shipped 5 a minute, and 60, 600 and every rate that divides 60,000, were exact, and those are the rates every test used. F165 was this rounding in the recovery budget, fixed there in V4-80; this file was not looked at then. Found by the eighteenth review. | v4 (V4-10b's limiter) | Low | `apps/authd/ratelimit.c` | **FIXED in V4-226.** The rate is reduced to whole thousandths per whole milliseconds and the bucket credited in whole steps, the clock moving by exactly the time credited: exact at every rate, with no state added. `test_authd_ws` `limiter:` asks ten times a millisecond for a minute at six rates and checks the boundary at 100 a minute (nothing before 600 ms, one token at 600). Run against the old file: red, with the numbers above. Campaign v136, RF1 and RF2; v50b's J5 follows the line. Erratum 109. | V4-226 |

| F315 | **On Linux the daemon's libsodium was built in, and linked from, a directory under `/tmp` whose name any local user could compute.** libsodium's libtool cannot build under a path with whitespace, so its source, build and install directories were placed under `$TMPDIR` for every tree, and under `/tmp` when `TMPDIR` was not set, which is the default on a Linux server. The name was `mldsa-auth-libsodium-1.0.22-<first ten hex digits of the MD5 of the build path>-install`: for the runbook's `/opt/build/mldsa-auth/build-release`, `/tmp/mldsa-auth-libsodium-1.0.22-c57c77fcd4-install`. The download is checked against its hash; nothing checked who owned that directory or what `libsodium.a` held when the daemon was linked. A local unprivileged user could create the directory before the first build, or re-create it after `/tmp` was emptied (a reboot, or systemd's ageing) with an archive of their own; the build's stamps still said libsodium was installed, and the next `cmake --build` (the runbook's upgrade, step 22) linked it into the daemon. The hardening and backend-symbol checks look at other things. macOS has a per-user `TMPDIR`, and CI runners have one user. Read, and the directory name reproduced by the new test; the substitution itself was not carried out. Found by the twentieth review. | v2 (Step 1's workaround for libtool) | Medium | `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`, `cmake/SodiumDirs.cmake` | **FIXED in V4-229.** libsodium is built and installed inside the build tree whenever the tree's path has no whitespace, which is every tree the runbook and CI make. A tree under a path with whitespace uses `TMPDIR` when the caller set it, the user's `~/.cache/mldsa-auth` when not, and is refused when neither is usable: `/tmp` is never chosen by default. The rule is one function of the build path, `TMPDIR` and `HOME`, and ctest `dependency_gates` asks it about seven hosts. Against the old rule: red, four checks, with the path above printed. A fresh tree at a path without whitespace, `TMPDIR` unset, was configured, built and tested to show the new place works. | V4-229 |

Against the old script: red on the edited and the added file. The four local trees and a fresh one are clean after a full build, so liboqs's own build leaves nothing behind. | V4-229 |

| F323 | **A dependency cache that was named but lacked a file was replaced by the network without a word.** Each of the three artefacts was taken from `MLDSA_DEPS_CACHE` if present there and downloaded if not. | F338 | **At accepted settings the rate limiter's table, not its rates, is what refuses.** The table has 1,024 entries, and an entry can be reused only when its bucket is full and it has no open connection. A new address that connects once keeps its entry for 60,000 / `rate_per_min` ms, and new addresses are admitted at up to `rate_global_per_sec`, so about `rate_global_per_sec` × 60 / `rate_per_min` entries are held at once: 600 at the defaults (50 and 5), 1,500 at `rate_per_min = 2`, 1,200 at `rate_global_per_sec = 100`. Past 1,024 every address not already in the table is answered 503 `limiter-table-full`, returning users included, and tightening `rate_per_min` makes it easier. Separately, `max_slots` above 1,024 (accepted to 4,096) cannot be filled by distinct addresses: the 1,025th is refused with slots free. Read, not driven. Found by the same review. | v4 (V4-10b's limiter) | Low | `apps/authd/ratelimit.h`, `authd_config.c` | **FIXED in V4-308**, by (b): the table is the caller's storage, sized at start as the larger of 1,024, `max_slots` and 1.25 × the addresses the rates can hold at once, to a ceiling of 16,384 (`ratelimit_entries_for`); the start logs `limiter-table count=` and `--check-config` prints `rate_table=` and warns when the ceiling binds (erratum 138). Review 66 had corrected the reason it was left open: no spec text or gate named the constant. A refusal at a full 16,384-entry table costs about 43 µs here against 5 µs at 1,024, measured, and less than a signature. Campaign v166 | — |

| F351 | **A reply could be written ahead of the `101`, or into the middle of a Pong.** The fix for F343 let the reply to a request go ahead of the Close echo "while nothing of the control bytes has been sent". | F352 | **`authd_client` dropped `--state` and `--proxy-v2` without a word over the raw listener.** Both travel on a WebSocket connection. With `--port` and no `--ws` the client spoke raw frames and used neither: `login --port N --state S` exited 0 with a login code bound to the empty state, and the site's exchange for `S` then failed with nothing pointing at the cause. A `--state` the daemon refuses (over 64 bytes, a space) got only "WebSocket upgrade failed", and `a&b` was sent as it stood and bound as `a`. Everything failed closed. Found by the twenty-seventh review, by reading | v4 (V4-13a's `authd_client`, found in V4-245) | Low | `apps/authd/client_cli.c` `parse_client_args` | **FIXED in V4-245**: either option without a WebSocket connection is a usage error (exit 2) that says to add `--ws`; `--state` is held to the daemon's rule (1 to 64 printable ASCII characters, no space) and to no `&`, with the rule named. Test: `client:` checks in `test_authd_cli`, with controls that the same options pass where they apply. Campaign v146 CS1–CS5 | V4-245 |
| F353 | **"Committed" in the browser's key store did not mean on disk.** The store's rule is that a staged key is durable before ROTATE is sent, and a new identity before the site is asked to enroll it; it resolved on the transaction's `complete` event. Browsers default to relaxed durability, where `complete` fires once the write has been handed to the operating system. A power loss or an operating-system crash in the seconds after could take the staged key back while the daemon had already committed the rotation: the browser then holds only a key the daemon no longer accepts, and the account needs a recovery code. A tab or browser crash is not enough. The command-line client syncs the key file and its directory. Found by the twenty-eighth review, by reading; not reproduced (it needs a power cut) | v4 (V4-13c's IndexedDB store, found in V4-247) | Low | `web/client/store.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-247**: every write transaction asks for `durability: 'strict'`. Test: `durable:` in `web_flows` drives the store's seven kinds of write through a stand-in database and reads what each asked for, red before (0 of 7); `browser_e2e` runs the same store in Chromium. Whether a given browser and disk honour the request is theirs | V4-247 |
| F354 | **A frame given to a closed socket was counted as sent.** `WebSocket.send()` on a closing or closed socket drops the data and does not throw. The session counted the frame, and the recovery probe reads that count: two frames sent and no answer is "the daemon refused this key". A connection that closed between ServerHello and ClientAuth was therefore reported as a refusal, and with both probes hit, as "neither key authenticates", when the daemon had seen neither. Nothing in the store changed on that verdict; the message was wrong. Found by the twenty-eighth review, by reading | v4 (V4-13b's transport; the probe's verdicts of V4-147, found in V4-247) | Low | `web/client/authd-client.mjs` `Session.send` | **FIXED in V4-247**: `send()` refuses a socket that is not open, counts nothing, and the probe's verdict is `unasked`. Test: `send:` in `web_flows`, with the open socket as control, red before for both the closing and the closed state | V4-247 |

Eighteen single changes to a constant passed the gate with the document unchanged, among them the user token lifetime cut to 1 h, the recovery-code limit to 1, the ticket lifetime to 30 s or 659 s, the line limit to 192 bytes. The recovery lockout's two constants were derived and never looked for. `AUTHD_MAX_RECORD` was computed from the script's own 9216 and 4096, so the content limit could change with its row and the record size beside it stay. | F361 | **A start that failed after creating the store used up the `store-created` warning.** The daemon opens the store, creating it if nothing is there, before it opens its listeners; the warning that a store was created (F199: `store_path` may name the wrong file) is logged only once the start has succeeded, and only by the open that created the file. A start that created a store and then failed (a listener that could not be opened, the descriptor limit, the log-privacy setup) left the store behind. The next start found it, was not its creator, and came up on an empty store with nothing in the journal; under the unit's restart-on-failure that is two seconds later. The one line on standard error went to the failed attempt. A store that was already there is never removed. Test: `authd_e2e` (the failed start, the store gone, the next start's `store-created`, and the control on an existing store). Campaign v147 FS1–FS3. Spec erratum 114 | V4-251 |

| F365 | **The reference site's WebSocket relay kept a client's socket for good once the daemon had closed.** The relay is two `pipe()` calls. When the daemon closed its side, `pipe` ended the site's writing side towards the client and stopped reading from the client; a client that kept its own half open was then held, paused, and stayed held after the client itself had gone, because a paused socket never reads the end. Every such connection cost the site one descriptor until restart: 50 connections left 50 sockets open fifteen seconds later, and at the descriptor limit the site stops accepting. No login is needed, only an upgrade the daemon closes. It applies where the site relays `/authd/v1` itself; behind Caddy the path never reaches it. F213 fixed the same hazard on the refused path only. Found by the thirty-second review, with a probe against the site's own module | v4 (V4-13c's relay, found in V4-253) | Medium | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` upgrade handler | **FIXED in V4-253**: when either side of the relay has closed, the other is destroyed a second later, as a refused upgrade's socket is. Test: `relay:` in `site_sessions`, a relayed upgrade the real daemon closes (a 65-byte state) with the client's half kept open; red before (`still open 5002 ms on, the site's side ended`) | V4-253 |
| F366 | **A logged-in session that logged in again, signed up or recovered left its old token valid at the daemon.** Those three routes give the session a new id and drop the old one with everything it held (F131). The daemon token was among what it held, and dropping it only forgot it: it stayed valid at the daemon until its own expiry, where `/api/logout` would have ended it. The token never leaves the server, so nobody could use it; it is the same loose end as a session that expires at the site (recorded, left), by another road | v4 (V4-58's fix for F131, found in V4-253) | Low | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` `retire` | **FIXED in V4-253**: the token is ended at the daemon before the session is rotated. Test: `retire:` in `site_sessions` asks the daemon about the old token after a second login and after a signup; red before for both | V4-253 |
| F367 | **With two session cookies the first decided, even when it named nothing.** The site read the first cookie of its name. Without `--secure` a sibling host can set a second `sid`; one that named no session, sent ahead of the visitor's own, made every request a new visitor: logged out for as long as the planted cookie lasted. Under `--secure` the `__Host-` prefix lets nobody else set it | v4 (V4-58's sessions, found in V4-253) | Low | `examples/site-node/server.mjs` `session` | **FIXED in V4-253**: every cookie of the name is looked at, and the first that names a live session is used. Test: `cookies:` in `site_sessions`, both orders, with two ids that name nothing as the control | V4-253 |
| F368 | **Common passwords that hold digits passed the passphrase policy.** 2,802 of the list's 9,913 entries contain a digit (`blink182`, `agent007`, `trustno1`). The "core" of a passphrase was taken by stripping digits and symbols together from both ends, so such an entry lost its own digits with the suffix: `blink182!?&+` was looked up as `blink`. And the word estimate looked substrings up only after undoing letter substitutions, which turns `blink182` into `blinki82`: `blink182trustno1` counted as sixteen characters. Both were accepted at about 62 bits, where `password!?&+` is `common` and `passwordpassword` is about 26 bits. Any entry followed by ten symbols: 2,165 of 9,913 accepted. The browser runs the same code, so it agreed. The class of F150, which V4-85 closed for entries made of letters. Test: `test_passphrase`, five new checks with a word-and-year and an unlisted passphrase holding digits as controls. Campaign v148 PW1–PW3. Spec erratum 115 | V4-254 |

| F379 | **`deploy/fetch-deps.sh` could not verify sqlite with LibreSSL and told the operator to delete a good download.** sqlite is pinned by SHA3-256, computed with `openssl dgst -sha3-256`; the only check was that an `openssl` exists. LibreSSL 3.3.6 (macOS's `/usr/bin/openssl`) has no SHA3 and prints its usage; the pipeline's status was `sed`'s, the hash came back empty, and the script reported `hashes , pinned ...` and "delete ... and retry" on every run. It failed closed. V4-314 looked at making that change and did not: a handle and a public key are not secrets, so anyone can send a signup naming an existing user's own handle and key, and `idempotent=1` would answer it `OK`. The spec (erratum 136), the unit, the example and the runbook's table state 4 KiB as x86_64's figure and what arm64 needs. Campaign v165 PG1; v51 Z3 and Z5 re-anchored. The shipped unit sets no `LimitAS=`, so this needs a strict-overcommit host. The runbook's "When it will not start" now names the line (V4-306) | |

| F456 | **A login in one tab could log the visitor out of it through another tab's request.** Since V4-253 (F366) a login, signup or recovery ends the old token at the daemon before rotating the session. Either answer, landing after the rotation's, replaced the new session's cookie: that session was orphaned in the table with a live token, and after `/api/recover` a fresh ticket was lost, a recovery code spent. Reproduced: a relay holds tab 1's `VERIFY` while tab 2 logs in; before the fix the answer was `Set-Cookie: sid=; Max-Age=0`. | F479 | **The upgrade parser read a request differently from the reader that framed it, and repeated headers as their last.** `ws_upgrade` ended a line at a bare LF and the header block at the first empty line; `conn_io`'s `end_of_headers` takes the request up to the first CRLFCRLF, so `...13\n\nGARBAGE\r\n\r\n` got a 101 and the bytes between were dropped. Two `Sec-WebSocket-Key` headers gave a 101 for the last (RFC 6455 11.3.1 allows one), and `Upgrade: h2c` then `Upgrade: websocket` was accepted. The server-side twin of F334. Reproduced by the review. Reachable only by a peer on the socket directly: Caddy and the site relay rebuild the request. | F480 | **The ML-DSA wrapper did not pin liboqs's sizes as the ML-KEM wrapper does.** `mlkem_wrap.c` fails the build if liboqs's ML-KEM-768 sizes differ from the wrapper's; `mldsa_wrap.c` had no such asserts, and `mldsa_verify` never compared the instance's key or signature length with its constants (keygen and sign each check theirs). Only a liboqs whose ML-DSA-65 sizes differ from 1952/4032/3309 could reach it, which FIPS 204 and the pinned commit rule out. Found by the fifty-fourth review, by reading | v1 (the crypto wrappers, found in V4-288) | Info | `src/crypto/mldsa_wrap.c` | **FIXED in V4-288**: three `_Static_assert`s on `OQS_SIG_ml_dsa_65_length_*`, and `mldsa_verify` refuses an instance whose public-key or signature length differs. Checked by compiling, in the native trees and the wasm build; there is no mutant, since the asserts check themselves at compile time and the runtime check cannot be reached with the pinned liboqs | V4-288 |
| F481 | **The spec called every strict-decode violation terminal; the client core keeps one session open on purpose.** An authenticated reply to `ROTATE` that is neither `ROTATE_ACK` nor `ERROR` returns the client core to LIVE, so it can still send `BYE`; the old key is already released, so no second `ROTATE` can be built, and the staged key is kept as for any unanswered rotation. §6's sentence read as covering both sides. Found by the fifty-fourth review, which judged it ambiguous rather than a defect | v4 (V4-13a's client core, found in V4-288) | Info | spec 6; `apps/authd/client_core.c` `cc_rotate_on_reply` | **FIXED in V4-288** (erratum 131), in the text: §6 says what the client does in that one case and why. No code change | V4-288 |
| F482 | **Vendored liboqs and SQLite were built without a stack protector or FORTIFY under a compiler that does not turn them on by default.** The project's hardening flags reach its own targets only, liboqs adds neither, and SQLite was compiled with `-w` alone, under a comment saying it kept "its own hardening". Ubuntu's clang turns neither on, so CI's `release (clang)` job, or an operator building with clang, shipped liboqs (which verifies signatures and decapsulates attacker-supplied bytes) and SQLite without canaries; `check_hardening.sh` still said canary=yes because the project's own objects import `__stack_chk_fail` (F104's class). Reproduced by the review: the pinned `sqlite3.c` under Ubuntu clang 18.1.3 imported no `__stack_chk_fail` and no `__*_chk`. Found by the fifty-fifth review | v1 (the vendored build since liboqs was pulled in; V4-7's SQLite, found in V4-290) | Low | `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`; `tests/check_build_flags.cmake` | **FIXED in V4-290**: every library target under liboqs's tree, its per-algorithm object libraries included, and `sqlite3` get `-fstack-protector-strong` and the project's FORTIFY definition (not `-Werror`); not the wasm build. `build_flags` now checks every vendored translation unit too, and fails if it finds none. Shown in an Ubuntu 24.04 container: clang 18 and gcc 13 Release builds pass `build_flags` (99 and 105 vendored units) and `check_hardening.sh`; under clang liboqs now imports `__stack_chk_fail` 22 times and SQLite once, where the old SQLite command line gave 0 | V4-290 |
| F483 | **The project's own `-D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=2` lowered Ubuntu GCC's default of 3.** GCC 12+ on Ubuntu defines level 3 itself; the explicit 2 replaced it for the project's code, dropping the `__builtin_dynamic_object_size` checks, while the vendored SQLite kept 3. `build_flags` enforced exactly 2. Reproduced by the review (Ubuntu 24.04, gcc 13.3: `-O2` defines 3, adding `-D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=2` gives 2). Found by the fifty-fifth review | v1 (the first hardening, found in V4-290) | Low | `CMakeLists.txt`; `tests/check_build_flags.cmake`, `tests/CMakeLists.txt` | **FIXED in V4-290**: the configure chooses 3 where the compiler has `__builtin_dynamic_object_size`, else 2, and `build_flags` is told the level chosen. Apple clang, Ubuntu clang 18 and gcc 13 all choose 3 | V4-290 |
| F484 | **The SQLite sources compiled were checked only when their archive was first unpacked, and the runbook said every configure checks both archives.** `URL_HASH` verifies an archive at extraction; an edited `sqlite3.c`, a cache archive changed after the first configure (step 22's upgrade path) or a `FETCHCONTENT_SOURCE_DIR_SQLITE3_AMALG` override reached the compiler unchecked, where liboqs's tree is re-checked on every configure. Reproduced by the review with the same `FetchContent` shape. Not a way in: whoever can write the build tree can write its objects. Found by the fifty-fifth review | v4 (V4-7's SQLite pin, found in V4-290) | Low | `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`; `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` section 1 | **FIXED in V4-290**: `sqlite3.c` and `sqlite3.h` are re-hashed (SHA3-256) on every configure against digests of the pinned archive's own members, taken from the archive; an edited file stops the configure, naming it (shown by appending a line and restoring it). The runbook now says what is checked when, including that libsodium's built tree is not re-checked | V4-290 |

| F506 | **The model's `state` proof held only because the attacker could never open a connection to the daemon.** The daemon's process read the ClientHello's `state` on a private channel only the honest page wrote, so the daemon answered only ClientHellos the device built; anyone can open `/authd/v1?state=X` (spec 7.1). With that connection added, P4 could not be proved: the attacker relays the device's ClientHello into its own connection, the daemon binds the code to the attacker's state, and the model's site, which took the session from the attacker apart from the code, logged the device in there. The code is safe because the page posts its code inside its own session; the model did not have that. Reproduced by the review with ProVerif 2.05. Found by the sixtieth review | v4 (V4-4's model, found in V4-297) | Low | `formal/authd.pv`, `formal/gen_controls.py`, `formal/run.sh`, `formal/README.md` | **FIXED in V4-297**: the daemon's process takes its channel, a second copy reads the ClientHello and `state` from the attacker, and the site reads code and session together from whoever posts them; the page's send on its connection is asynchronous (F406's lesson), so the attacks are exhibited as runs. All 13 results as before; `ctl_state` now targets P4b and is false; a new control, `ctl_session` (the old modelling), makes P4 false with the relay run. Twelve controls | V4-297 |
