| F511 | **The CLI's login can be phished the way a recovery code can.** `authd_client login --state X` binds its code to whatever `state` it is given, and the code is handed to the site by a person's paste. A look-alike page can show a user its own `state` at the real site (a session it opened there), have the user run the client with it -- the client connects to the real, pinned daemon -- and take the printed code, which then logs the attacker's session in at the real site as the user. The threat model calls logins phishing-resistant; for the browser it is (the page posts its own code in its own session, F506), for the CLI's paste flow it is not. Suspected by the sixtieth review; not run | v4 (V4-13a's CLI login, found in V4-297) | Low | `apps/authd/client_cli.c`; `docs/v4/threat-model.md`; spec 13 | **OPEN, recorded**: the threat model now names the exception. Recommended: the CLI prints which site the code is for, as the server identity it pinned, and the site's pasted-code form shows the session it will land in; or the CLI flow is documented as for operators who type their own state. The choice is the owner's. Corrected by review 66 (V4-306): the first remedy does not work, since the runbook pins `--server-id authd`, so the CLI would print `authd`, not a website; the reference site has no pasted-code form; and nothing documented asks a user to run `--ws --state`. Recommended now: document `--state` as for operators and testing only | -- |
| F512 | **Argon2id's working memory is not wiped, and the envelope key can be recomputed from it.** The pinned libsodium sets no clear-memory flag, so the KDF's region is unmapped uncleared; it is a plain mapping (`MAP_NOCORE` is 0 on Linux), not `sodium_malloc`, not locked and not excluded from dumps. During the unseal at start, about a second, a crash under a piped `core_pattern` or a swap-out writes it to disk, and with p = 1 the key is BLAKE2b of its last block: that key opens `server.ek`, the server key and, in a version-2 file, the store root key. The unit's comment said every key copy was covered, spec 12 that the derived key is wiped immediately. Reproduced by the review: 2042 of 2048 sampled pages non-zero at `munmap`, and the key recomputed from the last block matched. Found by the sixty-first review | v4 (V4-6's envelope reader, found in V4-299) | Info | `apps/authd/keyfile.c`; `deploy/mldsa-authd.service`; spec 12; `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` step 9 | **FIXED in V4-299** (erratum 132), in the text: spec 12 and the unit name the exception and what covers it, and the runbook requires encrypted swap or none. The residue itself is libsodium's; making the process undumpable around the unseal is left, recorded. **Root fixed in V4-305** (F531): libsodium is built to clear the region | V4-299 |
| F513 | **A stored recovery code was verified at whatever Argon2id parameters its row named.** `mrc1_parse` accepted any ops and memory and the matcher used them, as did the legacy `crypto_pwhash_str_verify` path: a row of `$mrc1$1$8192$...` verified at no cost against the 2 / 64 MiB policy (cheap to search offline, and F143's equal-cost refusal an oracle again), and `ops=400` took 0.73 s, so a larger value stalls the single-threaded daemon. Such rows come from a store written at other parameters, or edited; the envelope reader refuses parameters outside its bounds before any KDF work, the matcher did not. Reproduced by the review. Found by the sixty-first review | v4 (V4-76's matcher, found in V4-299) | Info | `apps/authd/recovery.c` `recovery_find_match`; spec 12 | **FIXED in V4-299** (erratum 132): a row is computed only at the daemon's own parameters; one that differs matches nothing, and a user whose rows all differ costs the one Argon2id every refusal costs. `test_authd_localapi` changes the daemon's parameters under issued codes (refused, one Argon2id) and back (accepted); F433's test now expects a user with codes to fail as the unknown user does when Argon2id cannot be computed. Campaign v163 RP1 | V4-299 |
| F514 | **A close at the daemon's own handshake deadline reads as a refused key.** The probe rule counts a close after `ClientAuth` and before the client's 20 s deadline as a refusal, because the daemon closes at once on a `ClientAuth` it does not accept; but it also closes every connection still in its handshake when `handshake_timeout_ms` (default 10 s, as low as 100 ms) runs out, without reading what has arrived. With the timeout below a slow client's round trip, every `ClientAuth` arrives after the close, and a probe reports "neither key authenticates", F402's wrong advice by another route; a Linux client suspended after sending `ClientAuth` (its monotonic clock stops) does the same. Nothing is deleted. Suspected by the sixty-second review, by reading | v4 (V4-263's F402 rule, found in V4-300) | Info | spec 10.2; `deploy/authd.conf.example` | **FIXED in V4-300** (erratum 133), in the text: spec 10.2 names the deadline close as one a client cannot tell from a refusal, and it and the example configuration say to keep `handshake_timeout_ms` well above the slowest round trip. **Not changed:** the daemon saying why it closed, which needs a reason the protocol does not have (owner) | V4-300 |
| F515 | **A failed wall-clock read was used as the time.** `app.now_unix = (int64_t)time(NULL)` had no check; -1 is before every expiry, so during a failure an expired-but-unswept token verified, an expired code exchanged, an expired ticket was redeemed, the sweep deleted nothing, each checked token's idle expiry was rewritten to 3599 and codes were issued expiring at 59. An earlier review had looked only at the monotonic clock's failure. On 64-bit Linux `time()` cannot fail in practice. Found by the sixty-second review, by reading | v4 (V4-8b's clock, found in V4-300) | Info | `apps/authd/authd_main.c`; new `apps/authd/authd_clock.h`; spec 12, 15 | **FIXED in V4-300** (erratum 133): a failed read keeps the last good wall time, advanced by the monotonic clock; with no good read at start the daemon logs `clock-unreadable` at ERROR and does not start. `test_authd_evloop` drives the rule with a failing read before and after a good one; campaign v164 WC1; v74 CK1 and v103 CK4 re-anchored | V4-300 |

| F531 | **In the browser, Argon2id's working memory stayed in the module's heap for the page's lifetime, and the envelope key could be recomputed from it.** The pinned libsodium sets no clear-memory flag (F512's cause), and its Emscripten build takes the 64 MiB region from the module's own allocator and frees it uncleared; wasm memory never shrinks. After a seal one block of the heap hashed, as BLAKE2b-256 of LE32(32) followed by the block, to the envelope key, and after an unlock two did, so a script on the origin at any later moment gets the key -- and with the sealed key in IndexedDB the ML-DSA secret -- without the passphrase, where spec 14 says the key is in wasm memory for one round trip. Reproduced by the review with node's own Argon2id computing the key independently. Found by the sixty-fifth review | v4 (V4-13b's wasm build, found in V4-305) | Low | `cmake/Dependencies.cmake`; new `cmake/PatchLibsodiumArgon2.cmake`; spec 12, 14 | **FIXED in V4-305** (erratum 135), at the root for both builds: libsodium is patched after its archive is verified so that `ARGON2_DEFAULT_FLAGS` is `ARGON2_FLAG_CLEAR_MEMORY`, which its own code honours; the patch refuses to build if the line is not where it expects. This also closes F512's residue natively. `web_flows` computes the key independently and scans the heap after a seal and after an unlock: red first (1 and 2 blocks), 0 and 0 after. Low because it needs script on the origin, which spec 14 already concedes is fatal while the passphrase is typed; it widened that window to the page's lifetime | V4-305 |
| F532 | **A reused `Authd` object answered one user's token with another user's identity.** The site's client keeps what the daemon has sent and not yet been asked for in a buffer, and `connect()` never emptied it. A list reply cut short by the daemon going away left three lines there; `connect()` again on the same object kept them, three requests were answered from the stale lines, and from then on every reply was three requests late: `verify` of bob's token resolved as alice. A second `connect()` on an open object also left the first socket's listeners attached, so its close failed requests in flight on the second. The reference site makes a new object per request and is not affected; a site that copies the client and keeps one connection, the case F249 was fixed for, is. Run against a stand-in daemon by the sixty-seventh review | v4 (V4-9a's client, found in V4-311) | Low | `examples/site-node/authd.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-311**: `connect()` empties the buffer and is refused (`already-connected`) while a connection is open; each listener ignores a socket that is no longer the object's; and a line that arrives with no request written ends the connection, so it cannot answer the next one. Low because the shipped site is not affected; in a copy that keeps a connection it is an identity mix-up. Tests in `authd.test.mjs` with a stand-in that cuts a list reply and one that sends a line unasked; hand controls NC1, NC2, NC6 | V4-311 |
| F533 | **`authd_admin backup` gave up after ten seconds while the daemon finished the backup.** Every socket command had one ten-second deadline, and `BACKUP` is the one whose time grows with the store: the daemon copies the database before it answers. On a store large enough the tool printed `timeout` and exited 1, the daemon completed and kept the file, a script read a failed backup, and its retry to the same name was refused because the file was there. From reading; the store size at which a copy takes ten seconds was not measured. Found by the same review | v4 (V4-9b's CLI, found in V4-311) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_cli.c`, `authd_cli.h` | **FIXED in V4-311**: `BACKUP` waits ten minutes (`authd_cli_timeout_ms`), and a backup that still times out says the daemon may be writing it and to look at the destination before retrying. The two figures and the sentence are checked by name; nothing waits for either. That the sentence is printed on a real timeout is not tested: it would take ten minutes. Campaign v168 AD4 | V4-311 |
| F534 | **`connect()` never settled if the socket was destroyed before it connected.** `destroy()` on a connecting socket raises `close` and no `error`, and the promise listened for `connect` and `error` only. Run by the same review | v4 (V4-9a's client; `destroy()` since V4-127, found in V4-311) | Info | `examples/site-node/authd.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-311**: the `close` handler rejects the connect promise (`connection-closed`), which is a no-op once it has resolved. Hand control NC3 | V4-311 |
| F535 | **A list command answered by one line that is neither `OK` nor `ERR code=` waited for `END` for ever.** The client took any first line not starting `ERR code=` as the start of a list, so `ERR internal` or a web server's `HTTP/1.1 400` left `listDevices()` pending, and everything queued behind it. No daemon sends such a line and the site sends no list command; this is F301's shape on the list path. | F540 | **`destroy()` and then `connect()` with a request in flight left every request waiting for ever.** V4-311 made each listener ignore a socket that is no longer the object's. The old socket's `close` arrives after `connect()` has replaced it, so it no longer failed the queue: the request in flight stayed at its head, marked sent, and nothing asked on the new connection was ever written. That is the sequence a site uses to reconnect after a hung daemon. Run against a stand-in daemon by the sixty-eighth review, which read the code changed in V4-306 to V4-312; at `31b6cbb` both requests fail promptly | v4 (a regression in V4-311's F532 fix, found in V4-314) | Med | `examples/site-node/authd.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-314**: `connect()` fails whatever was waiting on the connection it replaces and marks the object closed until the new one opens. Medium because a site that keeps one connection stops answering; the shipped site makes an object per request and is not affected. A test with a stand-in whose first connection never answers; hand control SJ1 | V4-314 |
| F541 | **`backup` waits ten minutes for a copy the shipped unit kills the daemon for after thirty seconds.** The copy runs inside one turn of the event loop and `WATCHDOG=1` is sent between turns, so a backup longer than `WatchdogSec=30` is a watchdog timeout: SIGKILL, every session dropped, and a destination file left that refuses the retry. The tool then sees a close with no bytes and prints the allowlist hint, not V4-311's. The unit's comment says the longest turn is under a second. From reading; no thirty-second backup was run. Found by the same review | v4 (V4-126's watchdog against V4-9a's `BACKUP`; V4-311 added the ten-minute wait, found in V4-314) | Med | `apps/authd/store/store.c` `store_backup`; `apps/authd/authd_main.c`; `deploy/mldsa-authd.service` | **FIXED in V4-315**: while the store copies, SQLite calls the store back about every thousand steps (`store_set_backup_tick`), and the daemon's callback sends `WATCHDOG=1` when a ping is due (`authd_sd_watchdog_tick`). The callback is installed for the copy and removed after it. `test_authd_store` counts the calls during a backup of 600 rows and none outside it; `test_authd_cli` checks the ping against a socket standing in for systemd's. Not tested: the one line in `authd_main.c` that joins the two, and a copy that really lasts thirty seconds. Logins still wait for the copy; the runbook says so. Spec erratum 142. Campaign v171 | V4-315 |

| F604 | **A correct recovery code with a space in or around it was refused.** The recover page trimmed neither field. A trailing space from a phone's keyboard, or a code typed in groups, was rejected by the site's own grammar before the daemon saw it and answered as `recover-failed`, at the cost of one of the client's five attempts; a name with a space after it was sent as another user. The code was not spent and the daemon's lockout not counted. | F609 | **Four more groups of mutations are killed by chance, and are not fixed yet.** From the same sweep. v103 CK4 and CK5 (the daemon's clock is stale after its sleep): a login is refused only if it connects late in the daemon's one-second sleep, and seven logins spaced by the client's own start-up, one key derivation each, are relied on to land there; estimated one run in a few hundred on a noisy machine. v101 DR3 (the drain ends before a computed reply is sent): the signal has to arrive while a handler runs; a signal in a window of tens of microseconds lets the mutant pass, and a very slow machine fails the clean suite too. v129 LW2 (the loop spins while a peer waits): a lower bound on elapsed time, which a stall inside the measured calls can meet; so can V4-326's new bound for v89 OE6. v71 EI1, EI2, EI4, EI5 (a secret is logged): the scan needs two printable bytes in a window of the secret, and about three random secrets in a hundred thousand have none | v4 (campaigns v71, v101, v103, v129, found in V4-327) | Low | `tests/authd_e2e.sh`, `test_authd_localapi.c`, `test_authd_conn.c`, `tests/journal_scan.h` | **OPEN for v101 DR3 only**, whose remedy needs the daemon to say how many replies were pending when it began to drain: a new log field, a spec change, and so the owner's. **The others were dealt with in V4-328.** v103 CK4 and CK5: one connection whose place in the daemon's sleep is measured (asked, 0.8 s, connected, one byte sent) and which a stale clock closes at once; a wait outside 0.65 to 0.95 s is taken again. After a block longer than that timeout the ClientAuth is refused as expired and the login fails as it did before, under another journal word (`client-auth-failed`, not `closed-deadline`). Only a connection still waiting to send its ClientHello is saved. The public can cause about a tenth of that duty through `RECOVERY-USE`'s budget; an enrolled user can cause nearly all of it through `RECOVERY-ISSUE`, which has none (F39, F625). Found by the same review, run against `evloop.c` with an injected clock | v4 (V4-332's F623, found in V4-333) | Low | `apps/authd/evloop.c` | **FIXED in V4-333 for the first**: a deadline is looked at before it is moved. **The second is recorded**: a cap on how far one deadline may be moved would close again the connection that asked for a long backup, which is F619. It is one more reason for the budget V4-332's entry asks the owner about | V4-333 |

| F644 | **An enrolled device can grow the store without bound by rotating.** A superseded key keeps its whole public key, in its row and again in the unique index, about 7 KB each, and nothing removes it. One valid device looping login and ROTATE at the default five connections a minute is about 50 MB a day from one address, with an audit row each; the state directory, every backup and every `audit-verify` grow with it. Recovery codes are never deleted either (the spec says so). And several statements scan whole tables for want of an index, 6 to 9 ms each at 200,000 tokens: not a cost today. Recommended: a minimum key age, a day by default, refused as rotation already refuses, and the indexes with the next schema change that is needed anyway |  |

| F653 | **The store's schema was never checked, so what was planted in the file ran inside the daemon.** `open_impl` creates what is missing and reads three rows of `meta`; it never asked what else the file held, and SQLite runs a file's triggers for whoever writes to it. With one write to a store or a backup and without the server key: a trigger that drops every `device-revoke` entry before it is inserted, so the revocation answers OK, no entry is written, later entries chain to the head before it, and the chain verifies over a history with the revocation missing, a valid extension of every head recorded off the host, which a truncation is not; triggers that set a revoked device's status back, so the revoke answers OK, is audited, and does nothing; a view named `audit` over the renamed table, so verification sees what the view lets through and every later append is discarded. A writer of the file can add a device in any case (the threat model says so); these survive the operator's response to that, and the audit chain exists to say what happened. On the connection triggers and views are switched off and the schema cannot be written, so what is planted while the daemon runs is not run and is refused at the next start. An append that changed no row is an error. Erratum 159. Two parts have no test that would fail: switching views off (a view is refused at open, and writing through one fails either way) and the changed-rows check (unreachable with triggers off). **V4-341 changed how it compares (F660, F661) and when (F664)** | V4-339 |

| F668 | **"This start created the store" meant "the store had no identifier".** The open wrote whichever of the store's three meta values was missing. The daemon takes "created" as leave to remove the file when the start fails (F361). The audit check's return was one of the few that did not remove, by accident of its place, and F657's remedy as first written here routed it through the epilogue that does: it would have deleted a populated store. A schema with no rows, which a creation cut short leaves, is still completed and is a creation. The daemon also never removes a store whose audit check failed, whatever the flag says; that second guard has no test that would fail. A file that was there and empty (zero bytes, or another program's empty database) still counts as created and goes with a failed start, keeping the mode it had while it lasts; the comment that said the file "did not exist" is corrected. Second: while the daemon starts, its handlers restart interrupted calls (F657), so a line already waiting when SIGTERM came was simply resumed: a daemon held from its first byte ignored two SIGTERMs and a SIGINT, and one that had seen the stop waited in the very line that says it was stopped before it had started. Under systemd that ends in SIGKILL with no epilogue, and a store that start created stays, which is what F657 was fixed to prevent. | F725 | **The key schedule keeps secrets in ordinary stack arrays.** `ss_x`, the PRK, the IKM and a session key are held in `uint8_t` arrays on the stack in `src/crypto/kex.c` and wiped before return. The protocol specification's Req 4.2 asks for secrets in memory from `sodium_malloc`: guarded, locked, never swapped. They are wiped on every path the review read; they are not locked while they live. Found by the same review, which read the protocol core against §4 to §7 and found it otherwise sound: transcript binding, domain separation, replay and expiry, the key schedule and the server's cost before a peer has proved anything | v4 (V4-1's key exchange, found in V4-349) | Info | `src/crypto/kex.c` | **Not changed, and recorded (V4-353).** Read again before moving anything: the four are scratch copies, each wiped on every path, and the values they hold are held on the stack in the same moment by the libsodium functions that compute them (the scalar multiplication's working state, the HMAC's). Moving the wrappers' copies to locked memory would cost an allocation or a process-wide buffer in the handshake's core and would not make "no secret outside locked memory" true. What Req 4.2 asks for holds of the secrets the protocol KEEPS: private keys, the two shared secrets, the session keys, each in `secure_mem`, which the `secure_mem_sites` gate lists. The claim map has said since V4-24b that a secret in an array on the stack is outside that gate. Whether Req 4.2's sentence should say "held" is the owner's: the protocol specification is not edited here | V4-353 |

Against a daemon built with campaign v190's RE2, the whole end-to-end test passed three times in three; a replica of the leg's statements showed the line kept 38 times in 40. On macOS the line is lost 39 times in 40 and RE2 is killed, which is where the kill had been made by hand. The nightly runs the campaigns on Linux, so RE2 would have survived there, and F720's fix had no test where it is deployed. Run here in a Linux container against both before the push | V4-350 |

The running daemon is not affected. | F728 | **A connection's deadline is acted on only when the loop next wakes, and the connection whose arrival wakes it is refused.** The loop waits up to a second, not until the nearest deadline; and it accepts before it sweeps. Six silent connections were each closed half a second after their deadline. With every slot held by connections past theirs, the next arrival was refused for want of a slot and the one after was served. A peer that holds slots gets up to a second more than is configured, and the first honest arrival after that pays for it. Found by the same review, which ran it on both systems | v4 (V4-8a's loop, found in V4-350) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_main.c`, `apps/authd/evloop.c` | **FIXED in V4-351**: the daemon asks the loop how long it may wait (`evloop_wait_ms`: the caller's cap, or the time to the nearest armed deadline if that is sooner, and nothing once one has passed), and the loop gives back slots that are past their deadline and have nothing to say this turn before it accepts. The sweep at the end of a turn is unchanged and closes the same connections; only when in the turn has moved, so a connection with a frame to serve at its deadline is still served first, as the unit tests pin. The one line in the daemon's main that uses the new wait has no test that would fail: what it changes is when the loop wakes, and no test here is decided by a clock. No erratum: the specification has always said a connection is closed at its deadline | V4-351 |

| F736 | **The browser enrolled a key against a server key it had never parsed.** The enroll page turned whatever the site sent as its server key into bytes, with a helper that took any string, and stored it as the pin before asking the site to enroll the new key. With an error page's text, an empty string, or `undefined` for a server key, the site enrolled a key and the browser kept it as confirmed; every login then failed at `server-key: bad-format`, and nothing re-pins. The site itself serves the file it was started with unparsed. Found by the same review, which ran four such answers | v4 (V4-13c's pages, found in V4-352) | Low | `web/client/flows.mjs`, `web/client/authd-client.mjs`, `web/pages/common.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-352**: `flows.enroll` has the module parse the pin (`checkServerPin`) before a key is made, so the site is not asked and nothing is stored; and the pages' hex helper refuses what is not hexadecimal. The site still serves its file as given: a wrong file there is now refused by every browser at enrollment, which is the place to find it | V4-352 |
| F737 | **Which account a new key went to was decided when the request arrived, and two tabs share the session.** One tab signs up as one name; before its enrollment arrives, another tab of the same browser redeems a recovery code for another account, which replaces the session with one holding that account's ticket; the first tab's key is then enrolled into the recovered account, and the page says so only afterwards. The other order spent a recovery code for nothing: a signup replaced the session that held the ticket. It needs two tabs acting within seconds. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-188's F267, found in V4-352) | Low | `examples/site-node/server.mjs`, `web/pages/enroll.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-352**: the page sends the account it means with the enrollment and the site refuses another (`another-account`); a signup on a session that holds a recovery ticket is refused (`recovery-in-progress`) and the ticket kept; the page also checks the account in the answer. Both refusals killed by hand | V4-352 |
| F738 | **What a script running in the site's origin can and cannot do, and what the passphrase fields ask of the browser.** The policy the reference site sends stops inline and remote script, requests to other origins, framing and form posts elsewhere; it has no say over navigation, so a script already in the origin can leave with data in a URL. Such a script can read the sealed key and the pin from IndexedDB, which are plain bytes with no WebCrypto wrapping, for guessing offline at the browser's Argon2id cost; the passphrase if it is there when typed; and it can use the session. And `autocomplete="off"` on the passphrase fields is not honoured by browsers for a password field: a password manager may offer to keep the passphrase. Found by the same review, by reading; the enroll page says most of the first | v4 (V4-13c's pages, found in V4-352) | Info | `examples/site-node/server.mjs`, `web/pages/` | **Recorded, not changed.** Whether the fields should say `new-password` and `current-password`, and so invite a password manager to hold the passphrase, is the owner's: it decides where the second half of the device's secret lives | |

Replaced with a constant, two new identities had the same handle. Only script already running in the origin can reach it. Found by the same review, which ran it under Node | v4 (V4-13a's module, found in V4-352) | Info | `web/client/authd-client.mjs` | **FIXED in V4-352**: made unwritable and unconfigurable once the module has initialised. **This fixes the property and no more (F747)**: the function behind it looks up the platform's generator on each call | V4-352 |
| F740 | **The gate on the daemon's writes to standard error was a reading of source by a script, and a fourth review found more it did not read.** With a whole directory of the sources missing, or unreadable, it printed OK: the search's failure was not looked at, and its floor counted files it had not examined. A macro defined after `usage()` that named `stderr` was taken for part of `usage()`. A dozen spellings passed (`write(` with its `2` on the next line, `writev`, `fdopen(2`, a string continued over a line), and the list of exempt files was by name, so a second `authd_cli.c` anywhere was exempt. The tree has none of them. Found by the ninety-fifth review, which ran each under three shells on two systems | v4 (V4-352's F733, found in V4-353) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_main.c`, `tools/audit/check_daemon_stderr.sh`, `tools/README.md` | **FIXED in V4-353 by not reading source for it.** The daemon's main, the one file with reason to print, now says after its includes `#undef stderr` and `#pragma GCC poison stderr perror psignal syslog vsyslog`: gcc and clang refuse every later use of those names, in a macro or out of one. Its last thirteen plain writes, all before the handlers, went through `authd_log_say` so that the file could say it; the poison caught a fourteenth use the script had never flagged, the log's own initialisation. The script now checks that the poison is in place, after the last include, before the first function and outside any conditional, and keeps a plain search of every other source for the name, with exemptions by full path and a failure if any expected file or directory is not found. What a search cannot see is listed at its head. Checked with a macro that names `stderr` under clang, gcc on macOS, and gcc in a Linux container | V4-353 |

No login by the named user is needed. The budget of §10.3 is on `RECOVERY-USE` alone. The family of F127, F343 and F351; F343's test had one request and a Close. No client of this project sends two requests ahead of a Close. Left as it is: a WebSocket violation behind a complete request in the same read closes the connection before that request is served, where the plain framing serves it first (F342); the peer there has broken the protocol | V4-358 |

| F766 | **A request refused in the same read as its PROXY v2 preamble was never charged to the limiter, and the journal did not say whose it was.** The loop asks the limiter about a connection once its preamble has settled, after the bytes read have been parsed. When the rest of that read failed to parse (an upgrade the daemon refuses, which any client can send through the proxy: a bad key, a second `state`), the connection was closed first: `closed-protocol`, with no `client-address` line, no token taken, no connection counted. And that is the usual case, not a rare one: a new connection waits a turn to be accepted, so the proxy's preamble and the client's request are both there at the first read (500 of 500 on macOS and on Linux). No signature is spent on such a connection; what was lost is the limiter's count and the address in the journal. | F787 | **A `ROTATE` that arrived late, and a second `rotate` in between, destroyed the only copy of the live key.** §10.2's rule was that `.ek` authenticating proves the server never committed `.ek.next`, so a `rotate` may delete it. It proves only that the server has not yet. A client that gets no reply gives up after twenty seconds and keeps `.ek.next`; the daemon's session lives until its idle timeout (60 s as shipped, 600 at most), and a connection that stalled delivers what it holds when it clears. A second `rotate` in that time probed `.ek`, found it live, deleted `.ek.next` and staged another key; the first `ROTATE` then landed, and the daemon's active key was the one just deleted. Nothing logged in again. No key is needed and no byte changed: delaying one flow is enough, and so is a network that stalls while the operator retries. The browser's store had the same rule. A staged key the passphrase does not open stops the rotation and is kept. The same in the browser's flows. Erratum 175. The end-to-end test is the review's relay; the browser's delivers the late `ROTATE` between the probe and the rotation's own login | V4-364 |

A `VERIFY` made meanwhile moves the token's idle limit back too, so setting the clock right ends that session as idle. F515 is a clock read that fails; nothing covered one that goes backwards. Found by the same review, with a preloaded `time()` | v4 (the store's expiry checks, found in V4-364) | Low | `docs/mldsa-authd-spec.md` | **RECORDED in V4-364, not changed**: §12 says it (erratum 175). Who can set the host's clock is root or the time service. A floor on the time used, kept in the store, would close it and would also refuse every token for the length of any step forward that is later corrected; refusing what was issued "in the future" ends sessions on each small backward correction a time service makes. Which of those an operator wants is the owner's; the recommendation is a high-water mark with a tolerance of a few seconds and a log line | V4-364 |

Found by the hundred-and-sixth review | v4 (V4-366's F794, found in V4-367) | Low | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c`, `apps/authd/authd_conn.h`, `apps/authd/authd_main.c`, `tests/test_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-367**: the real clock (`CLOCK_BOOTTIME` on Linux, else `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`) is `authd_conn.c`'s default, so a daemon that sets no hook reads it and `now_ms` is never the fallback; `test_authd_conn` judges a `ROTATE` 1.2 s after its code with `now_ms` held still and requires the refusal. Linux's choice of `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` is still not tested (it needs a suspend). Campaign v203's SD1 | V4-367 |
| F798 | **Section 6.3's place for the age check, before both signatures, was not tested.** The review moved it below them and `test_authd_conn` passed; a late `ROTATE` then costs two ML-DSA verifies, and the reply is `0x03` either way. Found by the same review | v4 (V4-366's F794, found in V4-367) | Low | `tests/test_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-367**: a late `ROTATE` signed by a key that is not the session's must be refused as late (`detail=rotate-late`), not as a bad signature. Campaign v203's SD2 | V4-367 |
| F799 | **Two texts were not true of the code.** `on_rotate`'s comment said the client discards its own key on the refusal; both clients keep `.ek.next` on any refusal. And `rotate-late` shares `0x03` with a key the daemon will not take, so the client's line and the runbook's bullet for that one can follow a request that was only late; the advice is safe for it, since it waits out the same ten minutes. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-366, found in V4-367) | Info | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c`, `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` | **FIXED in V4-367**: the comment and the runbook say so | V4-367 |
| F800 | **V4-366's record said a `ROTATE` is sent the moment the login code arrives; the browser's is not.** `ccw_rotate_build` unwraps the staged key with Argon2id after the code (seconds on Safari, F119), so with an `idle_timeout_ms` of a few seconds a browser rotation is refused as late with nothing holding it. The CLI opens both keys before the session. The default (60 s) is well clear. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-366, found in V4-367) | Info | `deploy/RUNBOOK.md`, `docs/decisions.md` | **FIXED in V4-367**: the runbook says what the browser needs of the timeout; the record is corrected in V4-367's entry | V4-367 |
| F801 | **The check bounds when a `ROTATE` is accepted, not when it commits.** Two verifies and `store_rotate_key` (a 250 ms busy timeout, then the write) follow it. The runbook's "can no longer land" still holds: the operator counts from the client's "no reply" line, at least 20 s (`LOGIN_TIMEOUT_MS`) after the code, and only a disk stall longer than that margin would break it. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-366, found in V4-367) | Info | (none) | **Recorded in V4-367**: no change; the margin is the client's own timeout | V4-367 |
| F802 | **The fuzzer never ran the late path.** `fuzz_authd_conn`'s zeroed application had no window, and its clock stood still. Found by the same review, by reading; it also noted that `conn_reset`'s `code_at_ms = 0` is an equivalent mutant, since the only way into serving sets it first | v4 (V4-366, found in V4-367) | Info | `tests/fuzz/fuzz_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-367**: the harness sets a 1 ms window on its own clock and moves the clock 1 ms a frame, so a `ROTATE` in the frame after the code is in time and one with a frame between them is late | V4-367 |
| F803 | **V4-366's busy-daemon leg did not make its case on a fast machine.** It kept the daemon busy with `RECOVERY-ISSUE` from three threads while a relay held the `ROTATE`, counting on the loop to move the session's deadline on by the time the hashing blocked. On GitHub's runner it blocked too little: with the bound taken out (SC3), the session was closed before the `ROTATE` was read, the leg stopped with "could not be made", and SC3 was reported a survivor by nightly 45646758088. The clean leg passed there, by the same margin. Found by that nightly | v4 (V4-366's F794, found in V4-367) | Low | `tests/authd_e2e.sh`, `tools/mutations/spec_v202.txt` | **FIXED in V4-367**: one `RECOVERY-ISSUE` is sent and the daemon is stopped (`SIGSTOP`) while it hashes; the `ROTATE` reaches its socket 3 s after the login code, the daemon is let go, and the handler, having outlasted its pass by the whole stop, moves every deadline on by it. Without the bound the `ROTATE` commits every time. Stopping the daemon in `poll()` instead was tried and does not do it: the wait times out and the slot is closed first. SC3's message follows the leg | V4-367 |
| F804 | **V4-367's stalled-daemon leg could fail on a clean macOS build.** It slept 150 ms after sending the site request and then stopped the daemon, without knowing where the daemon was. Stopped in `poll()`, macOS's `poll()` returns with nothing ready once its timeout ran out during the stop, though the `ROTATE` is in the socket, and the loop closes the session as past its deadline: the leg stops with "could not be made". Linux's `poll()` re-checks the sockets and serves it. Found by the hundred-and-seventh review, which reproduced both behaviours with a small program and failed the leg twice by moving the stop | v4 (V4-367's F803, found in V4-368) | Medium | `tests/authd_e2e.sh` | **FIXED in V4-368**: the leg waits for the daemon to log the request (`event=local-request cmd=RECOVERY-ISSUE`, written before the handler runs), stops it, and requires that the handler's count line (written after its hashes) is not yet there; otherwise it says where the stop landed | V4-368 |
| F805 | **V4-367's fuzzer change did not reach the late branch.** Once serving, any frame but an accepted `ROTATE` closes the connection, and the clock moved only for delivered frames, so a `ROTATE` was always one tick after its login code: in time, never late. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-367's F802, found in V4-368) | Low | `tests/fuzz/fuzz_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-368**: every chunk is a tick, empty and oversize ones too; a new seed puts an empty chunk between the login and the `ROTATE`, and two canaries require that the on-time one rotates and the late one does not (a hand control with a 5 ms window fails the second) | V4-368 |
| F806 | **V4-367's runbook sentence gave a wrong reason.** A `rotate-late` refusal means the idle timeout passed since that session's login code, not that ten minutes passed; and the line it named follows only a re-offer. The advice is safe for another reason: the re-offer's session began after the rotation that got no reply. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-367's F799, found in V4-368) | Info | `deploy/RUNBOOK.md` | **FIXED in V4-368**: the runbook gives that reason | V4-368 |
| F807 | **On Linux, a build without `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` would have fallen back to `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` without a word,** and the bound would not count suspend. glibc defines it under the build's flags. Found by the same review, by compiling | v4 (V4-367's F797, found in V4-368) | Info | `apps/authd/authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-368**: such a build stops with `#error` | V4-368 |
| F808 | **Two nits in V4-367's unit check.** The 1.2 s sleep ignored an early wake, which would make the late case a false failure, and `now_ms = frozen` assigned what nothing had moved. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-367's F797, found in V4-368) | Info | `tests/test_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-368**: the sleep resumes until done, and the assignment is a check that nothing moved `now_ms` | V4-368 |
| F809 | **A late-ROTATE leg whose Python was killed while it held the daemon stopped left the daemon stopped.** Both legs that send `SIGSTOP` (V4-365's relay, V4-367's stalled daemon) resumed it in Python's own clean-up; a `SIGKILL` (a group signal on a test timeout) skips that, and the daemon is the shell's grandchild, which its `cleanup` did not know. Found by the hundred-and-eighth review, by reading | v4 (V4-365's and V4-367's legs, found in V4-369) | Info | `tests/authd_e2e.sh` | **FIXED in V4-369**: each leg writes its daemon's pid to `pystop-*.pid`, and `cleanup` resumes and ends one that is still the daemon. Shown by killing the leg's Python with `SIGKILL` while the daemon was stopped: with the change the daemon is gone when the test ends; with the loop taken out it was left in state `T` (ended by hand) | V4-369 |
| F810 | **A daemon that died before the stalled-daemon leg stopped it was reported as a test fault.** `waitpid(…, WUNTRACED)` reaped it, the `SIGCONT` that followed raised, and the leg said "the test itself failed". Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-367's leg, found in V4-369) | Info | `tests/authd_e2e.sh` | **FIXED in V4-369**: the leg checks `WIFSTOPPED` and says the daemon exited, with its status | V4-369 |
| F811 | **The late-rotate canary checks only that nothing rotated.** On its own that would also pass for a ROTATE refused for any other reason; it proves the late path together with the on-time canary, whose bytes it shares bar one empty chunk. Found by the same review, which confirmed `detail=rotate-late` with logging switched on | v4 (V4-368's F805, found in V4-369) | Info | (none) | **Recorded in V4-369**: no change; the pair is the proof, and the review's log confirmed it | V4-369 |
| F812 | **The unit check's sleep loop would spin on an error other than `EINTR`.** None can happen with its constant arguments. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (V4-368's F808, found in V4-369) | Info | `tests/test_authd_conn.c` | **FIXED in V4-369**: the loop resumes on `EINTR` only | V4-369 |
| F813 | **Two refusals wrote no journal line.** `RECOVERY-USE` whose code was spent, or whose user went, between the check and the transaction, and `RECOVERY-ISSUE` whose user went before its write, answered `invalid` and logged nothing, where §8 has every refusal of a well-formed request logged. Only another writer of the store can make either happen: the daemon does not yield between the two. Found by the same review, by reading | v4 (found in V4-369) | Info | `apps/authd/localapi.c` | **FIXED in V4-369**: both log `recovery-use`/`recovery-issue` with the user and `detail=invalid`, as the other refusals do. Not tested: no test can put a second writer between the check and the write | V4-369 |
| F814 | **Issuing new recovery codes leaves a user's failure count and lock as they were.** A locked user's new codes do not work until the lock ends, and earlier failures count against them. Neither the spec nor the code says whether that is meant. Found by the same review, by reading `store_recovery_replace` | v4 (found in V4-369) | Info | (none) | **Recorded in V4-369, a question for the owner.** Recommendation: keep it, since a reset on issue would let whoever can ask the site for codes undo the lock, and say so in §10; that is an erratum, and the owner's | V4-369 |


Not re-listed: **keys at rest are unencrypted** (`demo_keys.c:349-352`,
`demo_keys.h:28-30`) and **no trust lifecycle** (`keystore.h:55-77`: no
remove/replace/revoke/persistence) — these are not defects to fix but the
substance of V4-6, V4-7 and V4-9.

## Coverage — first measurement

`tools/audit/coverage.sh build-cov`, whole suite (15/15), 25 profiles, project
sources only. Control: excluding the session tests drops `session.c` branch
coverage 95.99% → 76.28%, so the measurement responds to what it measures.

| File | Regions | Lines | Branches |
|---|---|---|---|
| `src/protocol/session.c` | 98.70% | 100% | **95.99%** |
| `src/protocol/keystore.c` | 96.08% | 100% | 92.96% |
| `src/protocol/transcript.c` | 95.99% | 100% | 88.79% |
| `src/crypto/kex.c` | 90.58% | 100% | 80.15% |
| `src/protocol/handshake.c` | 89.40% | 100% | 80.90% |
| `src/crypto/mlkem_wrap.c` | 82.64% | 100% | 70.33% |
| `src/crypto/aead.c` | 85.92% | 100% | 68.89% |
| `src/crypto/mldsa_wrap.c` | 81.31% | 100% | **57.69%** |
| `src/util/secure_mem.c` | 82.35% | 100% | 77.78% |
| `src/util/wire_int.c` | 100% | 100% | 100% |
| `apps/demo_keys.c` | 86.73% | 100% | 75.10% |
| `apps/demo_app.c` | 87.70% | 100% | 72.77% |
| `apps/frame.c` | 85.06% | 83.33% | 77.14% |
| `apps/net_io.c` | 75.07% | 93.75% | 68.65% |
| `apps/auth_client.c` | 77.25% | 66.67% | 62.99% |
| `apps/auth_server.c` | 76.81% | 50.00% | **61.17%** |
| **TOTAL** | **87.77%** | **97.19%** | **77.29%** |

Reading: the **protocol core is the best-covered code and the apps layer the
worst** — and the apps layer is exactly what the daemon replaces, so the
weakest-covered code is on its way out. `mldsa_wrap.c`'s 57.69% branches is
the lowest in `src/`: those are liboqs failure paths (allocation and
`OQS_STATUS` errors) that tests cannot reach without fault injection. Coverage
is **not** adopted as a gate — the mutation campaigns are the gate — but an
uncovered branch is one no mutation could be killed in, so these are the
blind spots.

## Spec-v2 conformance matrix — §4 security requirements

Machine-checked: that each cited test exists (script). Read, not machine-checked:
that the cited test actually pins the requirement.

| Req | Statement | Pinned by |
|---|---|---|
| 4.1 | No custom primitives | `test_vectors` (liboqs/RFC KATs) |
| 4.2 | Secret memory handling | wipe assertions in `test_handshake`, `test_session`, `test_net`; `test_session_alloc` |
| 4.3 | Constant-time comparisons | `tools/audit/constant_time_inventory.sh` (V4-1: 48 sites: 11 CT, 37 plain, all plain ones dispositioned). **V4-15b (F100):** the tree has 172 such lines -- 26 CT, which are 24 calls and 2 comments the script also counts, and 146 plain. None of the 29 plain `memcmp` lines compares a secret: they compare magics, protocol keywords, config keys, rate-limit address keys and public identities. The 24 calls are now a CTest gate, `tests/constant_time_sites.sh`. **V4-20:** 173 lines, 27 CT (25 calls), 146 plain -- the one new call is F48's owner comparison in the pre-check, listed in the same commit |
| 4.4 | Transcript-bound signatures | `test_handshake` (substitution, tamper, W-series) |
| 4.5 | Replay protection | `test_session` (contiguous-seq policy), `test_handshake` (ledger) |
| 4.6 | Strict input validation | `test_handshake` truncation sweeps, `fuzz_wire`, `fuzz_handshake`, `fuzz_session` |
| 4.7 | No silent key rotation | `keystore` refusal tested; **the "and logged" half is unimplemented (F9)** |
| **4.8** | **Build hardening** | **nothing — F17.** `tools/audit/check_hardening.sh` is the first check |
| **4.9** | **No secret-dependent control flow** | **nothing — F17.** No timing test exists |
| 4.10 | Build-time backend selection | `tools/check_backend_symbols.sh` + `bench.yml` agreement check |
| 4.11 | Decaps never a validation signal | `test_vectors` (implicit rejection), `test_handshake` H3 |
| 4.12 | Responder KEM secret wiped on every path | `test_handshake` H6 wipe matrix |
| 4.13 | Padding verified, not skipped | `test_session` P4; `fuzz_session` inner model |

**11 of 13 pinned; 2 unpinned (4.8, 4.9).**

## Debt ledger disposition (A1–A31 from v1–v3)

Blocking this deployment, each owned by a step: A4 rate limiting (V4-10),
A6 CA/revocation (V4-7/V4-9), A7 key storage (V4-6), A8 non-loopback listener
and hardening (V4-10/V4-11), A12 Req 4.7 logging (V4-9).
Moot by architecture: A3 multi-connection routing (one handshake per
connection), A5 thread safety (single-threaded event loop by construction).
Accepted and recorded: A9 traffic analysis beyond padding, A10 two-party only,
A19 v1's x86_64 deviation, A26 endpoint compromise, A27 both-halves break,
A28/A29 what fuzzing and benchmarks do not establish, A31 path-with-spaces.
Resolved in v2/v3 and re-confirmed here: A1, A2, A15–A18, A23–A25.

## What this audit does not establish

It is still **single-agent review**: the same agent that wrote v1–v3 audited
them. That is why V4-14 produces a review packet for an external
cryptographic review, and why this document lists what was machine-checked
separately from what was read. No timing measurements were taken (Req 4.9);
no side-channel analysis beyond reading for secret-dependent branches; the
pinned dependency versions were not re-checked against advisories in this
pass.
